Derivatives of Rankin–Selberg L L -functions and heights of generalized Heegner cycles
David T.-B. G. Lilienfeldt, Ari Shnidman

Abstract
. Let f f be a newform of weight 2 ​ k 2k and let χ \chi be an unramified imaginary quadratic Hecke character of infinity type ( 2 ​ t , 0 ) (2t,0) , for some integer 0 < t ≤ k − 1 0<t\leq k-1 . We show that the central derivative of the Rankin–Selberg L L -function L ⁡ ( f , χ , s ) L(f,\chi,s) is, up to an explicit positive constant, equal to the Beilinson–Bloch height of a generalized Heegner cycle. This generalizes the Gross–Zagier formula (the case k = 1 k=1 ) and Zhang’s higher weight formula (the case t = 0 t=0 ).



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
11G40, 11G15, 14C25

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Main result

Let
0<t\leq k-1
be integers, and let
K
be an imaginary quadratic field of odd discriminant. We consider the Rankin–Selberg
L
-function
L(f,\chi,s)
associated to a newform
f\in S_{2k}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
and an unramified Hecke character
\chi
of
K
of infinity type
(2t,0)
.
This
L
-series admits analytic continuation and a functional equation with global root number
-\epsilon_{K}(N)
, where
\epsilon_{K}
is the quadratic Dirichlet character associated to
K
. We further impose the Heegner hypothesis:

(1)

p\mid N\implies p\text{ splits in }K,

so that
-\epsilon_{K}(N)=-1
and
L(f,\chi,s)
vanishes to odd order at its center
s=k+t
.

Our main result (which is also Theorem
13.4
) is the following formula for the central derivative in terms of the Beilinson–Bloch height of a certain algebraic cycle
\Delta^{\prime}_{\chi,f}
:

Theorem 1.1
.

With the notations and assumptions as above, we have

L^{\prime}(f,\chi,k+t)=\frac{4^{k+t}\pi^{2k}(f,f)_{\mathrm{Pet}}}{(k-t-1)!(k+t-1)!hu^{2}|D|^{k-t-1/2}}\langle\Delta^{\prime}_{\chi,f},\Delta^{\prime}_{\chi,f}\rangle^{\GS},

where
h=\#\mathrm{Cl}_{K}
,
D=\Disc(K)
,
2u=\#\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}
, and
(f,f)_{\Pet}
is the Petersson inner product.

The class
\Delta^{\prime}_{\chi,f}
is constructed from an algebraic cycle
\Delta_{\chi}\in\CH^{k+t}(X)_{\mathbb{C}},
of middle arithmetic codimension on the generalized Kuga–Sato variety
X=W_{2k-2}\times A^{2t}
defined over the Hilbert class field
H
of
K
. Here,
W_{2k-2}{\longrightarrow\,}X(N)
is the Kuga–Sato variety realizing the motive of
f

[
33
]
and
A
is an elliptic curve over
H
with CM by
\mathcal{O}_{K}
. The cycles
\Delta_{\chi}
are weighted sums of the
generalized Heegner cycles
defined by Bertolini–Darmon–Prasanna
[
3
]
, and will be described in more detail in Section
5.2
. Roughly speaking, they are built out of graphs of isogenies living in CM fibers of
X{\longrightarrow\,}X(N)
, isogenies which “mix” the Kuga–Sato components with the constant components of the fiber. Let
\CH^{i}(X)_{0}\subset\CH^{i}(X)
denote the subgroup of homologically trivial algebraic cycles. The conjectural Beilinson–Bloch height pairing
[
1
,
7
]

\langle\;\;,\;\;\rangle^{\GS}\colon\CH^{k+t}(X)_{0}\times\CH^{k+t}(X)_{0}{\longrightarrow\,}\mathbb{R}

is well-defined on a certain subgroup
G\subset\CH^{k+t}(X)_{0}
, and
\Delta_{\chi}\in G\otimes\mathbb{C}
.
Let
\Delta_{\chi}^{\prime}
be the image of
\Delta_{\chi}
in the quotient of
\mathrm{Span}\{T_{m}\Delta_{\chi}\colon(m,N)=1\}
by the kernel of the height pairing (extended to a
\mathbb{C}
-valued hermitian pairing). The cycle
\Delta^{\prime}_{\chi,f}
in Theorem
1.1
is the
f
-isotypic component of
\Delta_{\chi}^{\prime}
with respect to the Hecke action.

Theorem
1.1
generalizes the formula of Zhang
[
41
]
(the case
t=0
), which is itself a generalization of the Gross–Zagier formula in the case
k=1
(and hence
t=0
)
[
22
]
. The second author proved a
p
-adic version of Theorem
1.1
in
[
35
]
, which was subsequently generalized by Disegni in
[
14
]
.

As in
[
22
]
and
[
41
]
, we deduce the following result about the analytic ranks

r_{\an}(f\otimes\chi):=\ord_{s=k+t}L(f,\chi,s)

of the complex
L
-functions
L(f,\chi,s)
.

Corollary 1.2
.

For
\sigma\in{\rm Gal}_{\mathbb{Q}}
, we have

r_{\an}(f\otimes\chi)=1\iff r_{\an}(f^{\sigma}\otimes\chi^{\sigma})=1.

If the height pairing on generalized Heegner cycles is positive-definite, as is expected, then we also deduce that
L^{\prime}(f,\chi,k+t)\geq 0
, as is predicted by the Riemann hypothesis for
L(f,\chi,s)
.

1.2.
The Beilinson–Bloch and Bloch–Kato conjectures

Let
Y
be a smooth and proper variety over a number field
F
. Taken together, the Beilinson–Bloch and Bloch–Kato conjectures
[
6
,
8
]
predict that for each integer
i\geq 0
and prime
\ell
, the higher Abel–Jacobi map

(2)

\AJ_{Y}^{i}\colon\CH^{i}(Y)_{0}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}\longrightarrow H^{1}_{f}({\rm Gal}_{F},H_{\et}^{2i-1}(\overline{Y},\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}(i)))

to the Bloch–Kato Selmer group is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
-vector spaces, and moreover these spaces have dimension
\ord_{s=i}L(H^{2i-1}(Y),s)
,
where
L(H^{2i-1}(Y),s)
is the
L
-function attached to the associated system of
\ell
-adic
{\rm Gal}_{F}
-representations.

In our setting,
L(f,\chi,s)
is a factor of
L(H^{2k+2t-1}(X),s)
, and so Theorem
1.1
yields the following implication predicted by Beilinson–Bloch–Kato:

Corollary 1.3
.

With the notations and assumptions as above, we have

r_{\an}(f\otimes\chi)=1\implies\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}\CH^{k+t}(X)_{0}\geq 1.

The Beilinson–Bloch–Kato conjecture is compatible with algebraic correspondences: if
M\subset h(X)
is a rational Chow submotive of
X
, determined by an idempotent correspondence
\epsilon
, then we expect that
\ord_{s=i}L(H^{2i-1}(M),s)=\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}\CH^{i}(M)_{0}=\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}\epsilon\CH^{i}(X)_{0}
. Combining Theorem
1.1
with work of Castella–Hsieh
[
13
]
and Elias
[
15
]
on Euler systems of generalized Heegner cycles, we obtain the following conditional result for the motive
h(f)\otimes h(\chi)\subset h(X)
:

Corollary 1.4
.

Suppose the map
\AJ_{X}^{k+t}

(\ref{eq:AJ})
is injective. Then

r_{\an}(f\otimes\chi)=1\implies\dim_{K(\chi)}\epsilon_{\chi,f}\CH^{k+t}(X)_{0}=1.

Here,
\epsilon_{\chi,f}
is the idempotent defining the rational Chow motive
h(f)\otimes h(\chi)
inside
h(X)
.
1
1

1

Scholl constructs
h(f)\subset h(W_{2k-2})
in the category of Chow motives modulo homological equivalence
[
33
]
, but injectivity of (
2
) implies that
h(f)
has the desired properties even in the category of motives modulo rational equivalence.

Castella–Hsieh
[
13
]
proved an analogous result in analytic rank
0
, under some technical hypotheses. See
[
11
, Rem. 1.2.2]
for further applications of our results to the Tamagawa number conjecture.

As the Bloch–Kato Selmer group of a rational Chow motive
M
depends only on its
\ell
-adic realizations
M_{\ell}
, we also deduce some applications to an a priori larger class of motives which should have useful applications.
Let
V_{f,\chi,\ell}
be the
4
-dimensional
\ell
-adic representations of
{\rm Gal}_{\mathbb{Q}}
attached to
f\otimes\chi
.

Corollary 1.5
.

Let
M
be a rational Chow motive over
\mathbb{Q}
such that
M_{\ell}\simeq V_{f,\chi,\ell}
for all but finitely many
\ell
. Suppose that
\AJ_{X}^{k+t}
is injective. Then for primes
\ell\nmid 2(2k-1)!N\varphi(N)
split in
K
, we have

r_{\an}(f\otimes\chi)=1\implies\dim_{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}}H^{1}_{f}({\rm Gal}_{\mathbb{Q}},M_{\ell}(k+t))=1.

The technical hypotheses on
\ell
again come from
[
13
]
.

Remark 1.6
.

If
Y
is a rigid Calabi–Yau
(2k-1)
-fold and
A^{2t}
is a power of a CM elliptic curve, then
h(Y\times A^{2t})
contains a motive
M
such that
M_{\ell}\simeq V_{f,\chi,\ell}
, by
[
18
]
. More elementary examples come from abelian varieties
J
isogenous to
E^{2k-1}\times A^{2t}
, for some CM elliptic curve
E
. When
J
is moreover a Jacobian, there is a canonical Ceresa-type cycle in codimension
k+t
, which one expects is typically non-zero in
\CH^{k+t}(M)
and hence in the Bloch–Kato Selmer group as well. See
[
28
]
or
[
5
]
for 3-dimensional examples of this type. Part of our interest in these examples is that they tend to give extremal behavior: e.g., the Jacobian
J
of the modular curve
X(8)
, which is the genus
5
curve with largest automorphism group. In these examples,
\CH^{k+t}(M)
is the
0
-th graded piece of the coniveau filtration on the Griffiths group
\Gr^{k+t}(Z)
, where
Z=Y\times A^{2t}
or
Z=J
. Note that the Tate conjecture implies that
M\simeq h(f)\otimes h(\chi)
, but the Tate conjecture is open in these cases.

One can deduce
p
-adic variants of Corollary
1.5
from
[
14
,
35
]
or
[
2
]
respectively, with hypothesis that a certain
p
-adic
L
-function vanishes to order
1
(resp. 0), and where the conclusion requires taking
\ell=p
. These
p
-adic results are unconditional since Nekovář’s
p
-adic height factors through the Abel–Jacobi map. Corollary
1.5
is the first complex result for motives of this type, but it seems difficult to make it unconditional at the moment.

1.3.
Strategy of proof

As in
[
22
]
, the proof of Theorem
1.1
is divided into four steps.

First we compute the Fourier coefficients of a newform
g=\sum_{m\geq 1}a_{g}(m)q^{m}\in S_{2k}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
satisfying

(3)

(f,g)=\frac{\sqrt{|D|D^{t}(2k-2)!}}{2^{4k-1}\pi^{2k}}L^{\prime}(f,\chi,k+t),\qquad\forall\;f\in S_{2k}^{\mathrm{new}}(\Gamma_{0}(N)).

This step is carried out using Rankin’s method to arrive at an integral representation of
L(f,\chi,s)
as the Petersson product of
f
with the product of
\Theta_{\chi}
with a non-holomorphic Eisenstein series. Using holomorphic projection, we compute the desired Fourier coefficients (Theorem
3.6
).

The second and third steps are concerned with the calculation of the geometric
q
-expansion

\sum_{m}\langle\Delta_{\chi},T_{m}\Delta_{\chi}\rangle^{\GS}q^{m}.

Injectivity of
\AJ_{X}^{k+t}
would imply that this
q
-expansion is a modular form as well, however this is not known and is only deduced as a result of our proof.
2
2

2

In the
p
-adic setting, the injectivity of
\AJ_{X}^{k+t}
is not necessary to deduce modularity of the analogous
q
-expansion of
p
-adic heights, which is one reason why this paper is technically more involved than
[
35
]
.
This means we must compute Fourier coefficients even in cases where
\Delta_{\chi}
and
T_{m}\Delta_{\chi}
intersect in the generic fiber. For each
m
, the height pairing admits a decomposition as a sum of local height pairings indexed by the places of
H
. The second and third steps are the calculations of the archimedean and non-archimedean contributions, respectively. Both steps are carried out using Brylinski’s pairing for local systems on curves
[
10
]
. The second step requires us to identify the Green’s kernel attached to the polarized variation of Hodge structures
W_{k,t}
described below. This occupies a large part of the paper (Sections
6
–
8
). The third step can be extracted from local height calculations performed in
[
35
]
, at least when there is no intersection in the generic fiber. In both steps, the case of intersection in the generic fiber uses Zhang’s work
[
41
]
on limits of local height pairings. The outcome of the second and third steps is the equality

(4)

\langle\Delta_{\chi},T_{m}\Delta_{\chi}\rangle^{\GS}=\kappa\cdot a_{g}(m),\qquad\forall\;(m,N)=1,

where
\kappa
is an explicit non-zero constant independent of
m
(Theorem
13.1
).

In the fourth and final step, we derive Theorem
1.1
from (
3
) and (
4
) using standard arguments.

1.4.
Green’s kernels

Generalized Heegner cycles give rise to elements in fibers of the weight
0
polarized variation of Hodge structures

W_{k,t}:=(\sym^{2k-2}R^{1}\pi_{*}\mathbb{Z})(k-1)\otimes\kappa_{2t}H^{2t}(A^{2t}(\mathbb{C}),\mathbb{Z})(t),

where
\pi\colon\mathcal{E}\to Y(N)
is the universal elliptic curve and
\kappa_{2t}
is the projector (
26
).
In Lemma
8.2
we show that the Green’s kernel associated to
W_{k,t}
(defined for
z=x+iy
and
z^{\prime}=x^{\prime}+iy^{\prime}
distinct in the upper half-plane) is obtained by averaging the function

g_{k,t}(z,z^{\prime}):=-Q_{k,t}\left(1+\frac{|z-z^{\prime}|^{2}}{2yy^{\prime}}\right),

where

Q_{k,t}(x):=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{2^{2t}dw}{(x+\sqrt{x^{2}-1}\cosh(w))^{k-t}(x+1+\sqrt{x^{2}-1}e^{w})^{2t}}

is a Jacobi function of the second kind.
As a result, the archimedean contribution to the height
\langle\Delta_{\chi},T_{m}\Delta_{\chi}\rangle^{\mathrm{BB}}
is a linear combination of sums of the form

\mathcal{G}^{m}_{k,t}(z,z^{\prime}):=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a,b,c,d\in\mathbb{Z}\\
ad-bc=m,N\mid c\end{subarray}}g_{k,t}\bigg(z,\frac{az^{\prime}+b}{cz^{\prime}+d}\bigg)(c\bar{z}z^{\prime}+d\bar{z}-az^{\prime}-b)^{2t}.

Let
\lambda=\{\lambda_{m}\}_{\geq 1}
be a relation for
S_{2k}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
, in the sense of
[
22
, p. 316]
, so that:

i)

\lambda_{m}\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\lambda_{m}=0
for all but finitely many
m
;

ii)

\sum_{m\geq 1}\lambda_{m}a_{m}=0
for all
\sum_{m\geq 1}a_{m}q^{m}\in S_{2k}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
;

iii)

\lambda_{m}=0
if
(m,N)\neq 1
or
r_{\mathcal{A},\chi}(m)\neq 0
for some
\mathcal{A}\in\Cl_{K}
.

As a consequence of our local height and Fourier coefficient calculations, we show that for points
z
and
z^{\prime}
in the upper half-plane with complex multiplication by
\mathcal{O}_{K}
, the value

\sum_{m\geq 1}m^{k-t-1}\lambda_{m}\mathcal{G}^{m}_{k,t}(z,z^{\prime})

is an algebraic multiple of the logarithm of an algebraic number, conditional on the non-degeneracy of the Beilinson–Bloch height pairing.
See Theorems
14.1
and
14.2
for the precise statements. This extends the algebraicity results
[
22
, V (4.3)]
and
[
41
, Thm. 5.2.2]
of Gross–Zagier and Zhang, which were recently generalized in a different direction by Bruinier–Ehlen–Yang
[
9
]
and Li
[
27
]
.

1.5.
Assumptions

It would be desirable to relax some of the hypotheses in Theorem
1.1
, especially in light of Remark
1.6
. Among other things, this would entail re-doing Gross and Zagier’s deformation theoretic computations on the modular curve
X_{0}(N)
in the presence of extra ramification. As the computations in the “minimally ramified” case turned out to be quite long on their own, we decided to focus in this paper on what is really new when
\chi
is no longer of finite order.

For the future, it would be worthwhile to prove analogous formulas for odd weight modular forms, in which case
\chi
must be ramified as well, in order for
L(f,\chi,s)
to be critical and self-dual in the sense of
[
3
]
. One could also try to allow modular forms
f
with “deeper” ramification, by combining the method of
[
32
]
with our work. It would also be desirable to relax the Heegner hypothesis (
1
), in which case one should work over a compact Shimura curve (corresponding to an indefinite quaternion algebra) as in
[
40
]
.
Generalized Heegner cycles over Shimura curves are well-studied
[
24
,
29
]
, but there are obstacles to overcome in order to compute their Beilinson–Bloch heights (see
[
38
]
). Finally, the case
t\geq k
(with global root number still
-1
) is quite mysterious. The arithmetic Gan–Gross–Prasad program for
{\bf SO}(3)\times{\bf SO}(2)
(and the results of Saito–Tunnel) suggest that one should work over the Shimura variety corresponding to a definite quaternion algebra. But the latter is
0
-dimensional, so this case seems to require a completely different construction of algebraic cycles. One promising approach would be to relate
L(f,\chi,s)
to modified diagonal cycles, as in
[
12
]
, by generalizing the triple product formula of Yuan–Zhang–Zhang
[
39
]
. However, this type of Gross–Zagier formula would necessarily involve other auxiliary
L
-values.

1.6.
Notations and conventions

By default, Chow groups are with
\mathbb{Q}
-coefficients.
Given a
\mathbb{Z}
-module
M
and a group
G
, we write
G_{M}
for
G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}M
. Likewise, if
E/F
is an extension of fields and
Y
is a variety defined over
F
, then we write
Y_{E}
for the base-change
Y\times_{\spec(F)}\spec(E)
. We use a similar notation for base-change of morphisms or algebraic cycles, though we sometimes suppress the subscript when the meaning is unambiguous. Starting from Section
5
, we adopt the following conventions.
In general, we will use the notation
z
for a point of
\mathcal{H}
, while the notation
\tau
is reserved for CM points. Points on
X(M)
will generally be denoted using the letter
Q
, while points on
X_{0}(N)
will be denoted using the letter
P
. The letters
x
and
y
will generally be used to denote real and imaginary parts of complex numbers respectively.

1.7.
Outline

Section
2
contains preliminaries on Jacobi functions, which will turn out to compute the relevant Green’s kernel for the height pairing.
In Section
3
, we carry out the
L
-function calculations. More precisely, we define the Rankin–Selberg convolution
L
-function, derive its functional equation, and compute the Fourier coefficients of the cuspform
g
(Theorem
3.6
). Section
4
recalls generalities about height pairings on algebraic cycles. Section
5
introduces generalized Kuga–Sato varieties and generalized Heegner cycles.
Sections
6
and
7
contain preliminaries on variations of Hodge structures and calculations of Laplacians, respectively. Section
8
is dedicated to constructing the relevant Green’s kernel needed for the archimedean Brylinski pairing calculations that are subsequently carried out in Section
9
. Section
10
computes the cycle classes of generalized Heegner cycles, paving the way to the calculation of the archimedean local heights of generalized Heegner cycles in Section
11
. Section
12
is concerned with the non-archimedean local height calculations. Section
13
puts everything together, giving a proof of Theorem
1.1
. Section
14
contains algebraicity results for special values of Green’s kernels. Section
15
provides a proof of Corollary
1.4
.

, which was written while AS was partially supported

2.
Jacobi functions

We collect some standard results about Jacobi functions using
[
16
,
36
]
as our main sources. We then prove an asymptotic formula near
1
for Jacobi functions of the second kind.

2.1.
Hypergeometric funtions

Given real numbers
a,b,c,x
with
|x|<1
and
c>0
, the associated hypergeometric function is defined by

(5)

F(a,b,c;x):=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(a)_{n}(b)_{n}}{(c)_{n}}\frac{x^{n}}{n!},

with
(a)_{0}=1
and
(a)_{n}=\Gamma(a+n)/\Gamma(a)=a(a+1)\ldots(a+n-1)
being the rising Pochhammer symbol. These functions satisfy Euler’s transformation law

(6)

F(a,b,c;x)=(1-x)^{c-a-b}F(c-a,c-b,c;x).

2.2.
Jacobi polynomials

Let
\alpha
and
\beta
be real numbers, and let
n\geq 0
be an integer. The corresponding Jacobi polynomial
P_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)}
is defined by the formula

(7)

P_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)}(x):=\frac{(\alpha+1)_{n}}{n!}F\left(-n,n+\alpha+\beta+1,\alpha+1;\frac{1-x}{2}\right).

Explicitly, we have

(8)

P_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)}(x)=\frac{\Gamma(n+\alpha+1)}{n!\Gamma(n+\alpha+\beta+1)}\sum_{j=0}^{n}\binom{n}{j}\frac{\Gamma(n+\alpha+\beta+j+1)}{\Gamma(\alpha+j+1)}\left(\frac{x-1}{2}\right)^{j}.

The following formula is known as Rodrigues’ formula
[
16
, Vol. II 10.8.(10)]
:

(9)

P_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)}(x)=\frac{(-1)^{n}}{2^{n}n!(1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta}}\left(\frac{d}{dx}\right)^{n}[(1-x)^{n+\alpha}(1+x)^{n+\beta}].

The Jacobi polynomials are solutions of the second order differential equation
[
16
, Vol. II 10.8.(14)]

(10)

(1-x^{2})f^{\prime\prime}(x)+[\beta-\alpha-(\alpha+\beta+2)x]f^{\prime}(x)+n(n+\alpha+\beta+1)f(x)=0.

For
0\leq t\leq k-1
, define the function

(11)

P_{k,t}(x)=\frac{1}{2^{k-t-1}(k+t-1)!}\left(\frac{d}{dx}\right)^{k+t-1}[(x^{2}-1)^{k-t-1}(x-1)^{2t}].

Proposition 2.1
.

For
0\leq t\leq k-1
, we have
P_{k,t}(x)=P_{k-t-1}^{(0,2t)}(x)
.

Proof.

Observe, using Rodrigues’s formula (
9
), that

P_{k+t-1}^{(0,-2t)}(x)=\frac{(-1)^{k+t-1}(1+x)^{2t}}{2^{k+t-1}(k+t-1)!}\left(\frac{d}{dx}\right)^{k+t-1}[(1-x)^{k+t-1}(1+x)^{k-t-1}]=\frac{(1+x)^{2t}}{2^{2t}}P_{k,t}(x).

Thus, using (
8
) and Euler’s tranformation law (
6
), we see that

P_{k,t}(x)=\frac{2^{2t}}{(1+x)^{2t}}F\left(-k-t+1,k-t,1;\frac{1-x}{2}\right)=F\left(k+t,-k+t+1,1;\frac{1-x}{2}\right)=P_{k-t-1}^{(0,2t)}(x).

∎

Associated with the polynomials
P_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)}
are the Jacobi polynomials of the second kind
3
3

3

We depart here from the notations of
[
16
]
where these polynomials are denoted
q_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)}(x)

[
16
, Vol. II 10.8.(24)]
. Instead, we adopt the notations of
[
19
, (9)]
, except that the polynomials therein are normalized by a factor of
\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta+2)}{2^{\alpha+\beta+1}\Gamma(\alpha+1)\Gamma(\beta+1)}
which we choose to drop.

(12)

W_{n-1}^{(\alpha,\beta)}(x):=\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{P^{(\alpha,\beta)}_{n}(x)-P^{(\alpha,\beta)}_{n}(u)}{x-u}(1-u)^{\alpha}(1+u)^{\beta}du,

defined for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, all
x\in\mathbb{C}
, and
\alpha,\beta>-1
. The function (
12
) is the degree
n-1
polynomial solution of the inhomogeneous linear differential equation

(1-x^{2})f^{\prime\prime}(x)+[\alpha-\beta+(\alpha+\beta-2)x]f^{\prime}(x)+(n+1)(n+\alpha+\beta)f(x)=2(\alpha+\beta+1)\frac{d}{dx}P_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)}(x).

2.3.
Jacobi functions of the second kind

The Jacobi functions of the second kind are denoted by
Q^{(\alpha,\beta)}_{n}
and defined, for
x
in the complex plane cut along the segment
[-1,1]
, by the formula

[
36
, (4.61.1))]

(13)

Q^{(\alpha,\beta)}_{n}(x):=\frac{2^{-n-1}}{(x-1)^{\alpha}(x+1)^{\beta}}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{(1-u)^{n+\alpha}(1+u)^{n+\beta}}{(x-u)^{n+1}}du.

In the special case
\alpha=\beta=0
, the function
Q_{n}(x):=Q_{n}^{(0,0)}(x)
is the Legendre function of the second kind. The function
Q_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)}(x)
can be defined for
-1<x<1
by the formula
[
36
, (4.62.9)]
, but this definition will not be needed here. The function
Q_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)}(x)
is a non-polynomial solution of the differential equation (
10
), which is linearly independent of
P_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)}(x)

[
36
, Thm. 4.61.1]
. According to
[
36
, Thm. 4.61.2]
, the following representations hold for
x
in the complex plane cut along the segment
[-1,1]
:

(14)

Q_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)}(x)=\frac{2^{-1}}{(x-1)^{\alpha}(x+1)^{\beta}}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{P^{(\alpha,\beta)}_{n}(u)}{x-u}(1-u)^{\alpha}(1+u)^{\beta}du,

and

(15)

Q_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)}(x):=\frac{2^{n+\alpha+\beta}\Gamma(n+\alpha+1)\Gamma(n+\beta+1)}{\Gamma(2n+\alpha+\beta+2)(x-1)^{n+\alpha+1}(x+1)^{\beta}}F\left(n+1,n+\alpha+1,2n+\alpha+\beta+2;\frac{2}{1-x}\right).

Observe, using (
14
) and (
12
), that for
x
in the complex plane cut along the segment
[-1,1]
we have

\displaystyle Q_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{-1}}{(x-1)^{\alpha}(x+1)^{\beta}}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{P^{(\alpha,\beta)}_{n}(u)-P^{(\alpha,\beta)}_{n}(x)+P^{(\alpha,\beta)}_{n}(x)}{x-u}(1-u)^{\alpha}(1+u)^{\beta}du

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{-1}P^{(\alpha,\beta)}_{n}(x)}{(x-1)^{\alpha}(x+1)^{\beta}}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{(1-u)^{\alpha}(1+u)^{\beta}}{x-u}du-\frac{2^{-1}W_{n-1}^{(\alpha,\beta)}(x)}{(x-1)^{\alpha}(x+1)^{\beta}}.

Using (
13
), we obtain

(16)

Q_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)}(x)=P^{(\alpha,\beta)}_{n}(x)Q_{0}^{(\alpha,\beta)}(x)-\frac{2^{-1}W_{n-1}^{(\alpha,\beta)}(x)}{(x-1)^{\alpha}(x+1)^{\beta}},\qquad\forall x\not\in[-1,1].

Equation (
16
) is
[
16
, Vol. II 10.8.(25)]
, given our notations and normalizations.

For all
0\leq t\leq k-1
, we define the function

(17)

Q_{k,t}(x)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{2^{2t}dw}{(x+\sqrt{x^{2}-1}\cosh(w))^{k-t}(x+1+\sqrt{x^{2}-1}e^{w})^{2t}},

where
x
is in the complex plane cut along the segment
[-1,1]
.

Proposition 2.2
.

For
0\leq t\leq k-1
, we have

Q_{k,t}(x)=2Q^{(0,2t)}_{k-t-1}(x).

Proof.

Replacing
\cosh(w)=(e^{2w}+1)/(2e^{w})
in (
17
) and performing the change of variables
v=\sqrt{\frac{x+1}{x-1}}e^{w}
gives the formula

Q_{k,t}(x)=2^{k+t}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{v^{k-t-1}dv}{(2xv+(x-1)v^{2}+x+1)^{k-t}(x+1+(x-1)v)^{2t}}.

Rewriting
2xv+(x-1)v^{2}+x+1=(v+1)(x(v+1)+1-v)
and performing the change of variables
v=\frac{1+u}{1-u}
yields

Q_{k,t}(x)=2^{-k+t+1}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{(1+u)^{k-t-1}(1-u)^{k+t-1}du}{(x-u)^{k+t}}.

By comparing with (
13
), we observe that

Q_{k,t}(x)=\frac{2^{2t+1}}{(x+1)^{2t}}Q_{k+t-1}^{(0,-2t)}(x).

Using the defining equation (
15
) leads to

Q_{k,t}(x)=2^{k+t}\frac{\Gamma(k+t)\Gamma(k-t)}{\Gamma(2k)(x-1)^{k+t}}F\left(k+t,k+t,2k;\frac{2}{1-x}\right).

Applying Euler’s transformation law (
6
) yields

\displaystyle Q_{k,t}(x)

\displaystyle=2^{k+t}\frac{\Gamma(k+t)\Gamma(k-t)}{\Gamma(2k)(x-1)^{k+t}}\left(1-\frac{2}{1-x}\right)^{-2t}F\left(k-t,k-t,2k;\frac{2}{1-x}\right)

\displaystyle=2\left(2^{k+t-1}\frac{\Gamma(k+t)\Gamma(k-t)}{\Gamma(2k)(x-1)^{k-t}(x+1)^{2t}}F\left(k-t,k-t,2k;\frac{2}{1-x}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=2Q_{k-t-1}^{(0,2t)}(x),

where we used (
15
) in the last equality.
∎

Corollary 2.3
.

The function
Q_{k,t}(x)
is a solution of the second order differential equation

(1-x^{2})f^{\prime\prime}(x)+[2t-(2t+2)x]f^{\prime}(x)+(k-t-1)(k+t)f(x)=0.

Proof.

It follows from Proposition
2.2
and the fact that
Q_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)}(x)
is a solution of (
10
).
∎

2.4.
Asymptotics for Jacobi functions

Lemma 2.4
.

For
0\leq t\leq k-1
, we have

Q_{k,t}(x)=O(x^{-k-t}),\qquad\text{ as }x\to+\infty.

Proof.

By (
15
), and using the definition of the hypergeometric function (
5
), we have

Q_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)}(x)=\frac{(-1)^{n+1}2^{\alpha+\beta-1}}{(x-1)^{\alpha}(x+1)^{\beta}}\sum_{j\geq 0}\binom{n+j}{j}\frac{\Gamma(n+\alpha+j+1)\Gamma(n+\beta+1)}{\Gamma(2n+\alpha+\beta+j+2)}\left(\frac{2}{1-x}\right)^{n+j+1}.

Thus,
Q_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)}(x)=O(x^{-n-\alpha-\beta-1})
as
x\to+\infty
. In particular, using Proposition
2.2
, we see that
Q_{k,t}(x)=O(x^{-(k-t-1+2t+1)})=O(x^{-(k+t)})
as
x\to+\infty
.
∎

Lemma 2.5
.

For
0\leq t\leq k-1
, we have

Q_{k,t}(x)=\log\left(\frac{x+1}{x-1}\right)-\left(\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\Gamma}(k+t)-\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\Gamma}(1)+\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\Gamma}(k-t)-\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\Gamma}(1)\right)+o(1),\qquad\text{ as }x\to 1^{+}.

Proof.

Let
n:=k-t-1
in this proof.
Using Proposition
2.2
, (
16
), and (
13
), for all
x\in\mathbb{C}\setminus[-1,1]
we have

(18)

Q_{k,t}(x)=\frac{P^{(0,2t)}_{n}(x)}{(x+1)^{2t}}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{(1+u)^{2t}}{x-u}du-\frac{W_{n-1}^{(0,2t)}(x)}{(x+1)^{2t}}.

By
[
19
, (87)]
, for all
x\in\mathbb{C}
, we have
4
4

4

Beware of our normalizations that differ from the ones in
[
19
]
.

(19)

\frac{2t+1}{2^{2t+1}}W^{(0,2t)}_{n-1}(x)=\frac{(2t+1)(k+t)}{2}\sum_{j=0}^{k-t-2}\frac{P^{(0,-2t)}_{j}(x)P^{(0,2t)}_{k-t-2-j}(x)}{(j+1)(k+t-1-j)}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\frac{W^{(0,2t)}_{n-1}(1)}{2^{2t}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{k-t-2}\frac{k+t}{(j+1)(k+t-1-j)}=\sum_{j=0}^{k-t-2}\left(\frac{1}{j+1}+\frac{1}{k+t-1-j}\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{k-t-1}\frac{1}{j}+\sum_{j=2t+1}^{k+t-1}\frac{1}{j}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\Gamma}(k-t)-\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\Gamma}(1)\right)+\left(\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\Gamma}(k+t)-\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\Gamma}(1)\right)-\left(\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\Gamma}(2t+1)-\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\Gamma}(1)\right).

We now calculate the elementary integral in (
18
):

\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{(1+u)^{2t}}{x-u}du=(1+x)^{2t}\log\left(\frac{x+1}{x-1}\right)+\sum_{j=1}^{2t}\binom{2t}{j}\frac{(-1)^{j}}{j}(x+1)^{2t-j}[(x+1)^{j}-(x-1)^{j}].

As
x\to 1^{+}
, we thus obtain

Q_{k,t}(x)=\log\left(\frac{x+1}{x-1}\right)+\sum_{j=1}^{2t}\binom{2t}{j}\frac{(-1)^{j}}{j}\\
-\left(\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\Gamma}(k-t)-\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\Gamma}(1)\right)-\left(\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\Gamma}(k+t)-\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\Gamma}(1)\right)+\left(\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\Gamma}(2t+1)-\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\Gamma}(1)\right)+o(1).

It is a standard fact about harmonic numbers that the formula

(20)

\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\Gamma}(2t+1)-\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\Gamma}(1)=\sum_{j=1}^{2t}\frac{1}{j}=-\sum_{j=1}^{2t}\binom{2t}{j}\frac{(-1)^{j}}{j}

holds (an easy check using integral representations). This last observation concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 2.6
.

When
t=0
, Lemma
2.5
recovers the asymptotic formula
[
22
, p. 251]
for Legendre functions of the second kind (note that the
O(1)
should be
o(1)
therein).

3.
L
-functions

Let
r=2k\geq 2
and
N\geq 3
be fixed integers. Let
K
denote an imaginary quadratic field of odd discriminant
D
prime to
N
. Consider

f=\sum_{n\geq 1}a_{f}(n)q^{n}\in S_{r}^{\new}(\Gamma_{0}(N)),

and let
\chi\colon\mathbb{A}_{K}^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
be a Hecke character of
K
of infinity type
(\ell,0)
, with
0<\ell\leq r-2
. We assume that the Dirichlet character
\chi_{|\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{\times}}\cdot\mathrm{Nm}^{-\ell}
is trivial, and hence
\chi^{\tau}\chi
is an even power of the norm, where
{\rm Gal}(K/\mathbb{Q})=\langle\tau\rangle
. This forces
\ell
to be even, so
\ell=2t
.
We will also assume that
\chi
is unramified. There are then at most
\#\Cl_{K}
characters
\chi
of type
(\ell,0)
, all differing by characters which factor through
\Cl_{K}\simeq{\rm Gal}(H/K)
, where
H
is the Hilbert class field.

Associated to
\chi
is the theta series
[
2
, Prop. 3.13]

\Theta_{\chi}=\sum_{\mathfrak{a}\trianglelefteq\mathcal{O}_{K}}\chi(\mathfrak{a})q^{N(\mathfrak{a})}\in S_{2t+1}(\Gamma_{0}(|D|),\epsilon_{K}).

Given an ideal class
\mathcal{A}\in\Cl_{K}
, there are also partial theta series

\Theta_{\mathcal{A},\chi}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathfrak{a}\trianglelefteq\mathcal{O}_{K}\\
[\mathfrak{a}]=\mathcal{A}\end{subarray}}\chi(\mathfrak{a})q^{N(\mathfrak{a})}=\sum_{n\geq 1}r_{\mathcal{A},\chi}(n)q^{n}\in S_{2t+1}(\Gamma_{0}(|D|),\epsilon_{K}),

where

r_{\mathcal{A},\chi}(n)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[\mathfrak{a}]=\mathcal{A}\\
N(\mathfrak{a})=n\end{subarray}}\chi(\mathfrak{a}).

We have the relation

\Theta_{\chi}=\sum_{\mathcal{A}\in\Cl_{K}}\Theta_{\mathcal{A},\chi}.

3.1.
Rankin–Selberg convolution
L
-functions

Let
\mathcal{A}\in\Cl_{K}
be an ideal class, and consider the Rankin–Selberg convolution
L
-function of
f
and the partial theta series
\Theta_{\mathcal{A},\chi}
defined by

L_{\mathcal{A}}(f,\chi,s):=L^{(N)}(\epsilon_{K},2s-2k-2t+1)\sum_{n\geq 1}a_{f}(n)r_{\mathcal{A},\chi}(n)n^{-s},

where
\epsilon_{K}=\epsilon_{D}
is the quadratic character associated to
K
.
It converges locally uniformly on the right half-plane
\Re(s)>k+t+3/2
of the complex plane. The Rankin–Selberg convolution
L
-function of
f
and
\Theta_{\chi}
is then given by

L(f,\chi,s)=\sum_{\mathcal{A}\in\Cl_{K}}L_{\mathcal{A}}(f,\chi,s).

Define the completed
L
-function

L^{*}_{\mathcal{A}}(f,\chi,s):=(2\pi)^{-s}N^{s}D^{s}\Gamma(s)\Gamma(s-2t)L_{\mathcal{A}}(f,\chi,s).

Theorem 3.1
.

The
L
-function
L^{*}_{\mathcal{A}}(f,\chi,s)
admits an analytic continuation to the whole complex plane and satisfies the functional equation

L^{*}_{\mathcal{A}}(f,\chi,s)=-\epsilon_{K}(N)L^{*}_{\mathcal{A}}(f,\chi,2k+2t-s).

Proof.

The key is to use Rankin’s method to prove the equality

(21)

\frac{N^{s}\Gamma(s+2k-1)}{(4\pi)^{s+2k-1}}L_{\mathcal{A}}(f,\chi,s+2k-1)=(f,\tilde{\Phi}_{\bar{s}})_{\Gamma_{0}(N)},

where
\tilde{\Phi}_{s}
is a non-holomorphic modular form of weight
2k
and level
\Gamma_{0}(N)
given by the trace of the product of the theta function
\Theta_{\bar{\mathcal{A}},\chi}
with a non-holomorphic Eisenstein family indexed by
s
:

\tilde{\Phi}_{s}=\mathrm{Tr}^{ND}_{N}(E_{s}^{(1)}(Nz)\Theta_{\bar{\mathcal{A}},\chi}(z)),

where

E_{s}^{(1)}(z)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}c,d\in\mathbb{Z}\\
D|c\end{subarray}}\frac{\epsilon_{K}(d)}{(cz+d)^{2k-2t-1}}\frac{y^{s}}{|cz+d|^{2s}}.

By explicitly working out an expression for the trace, we obtain

\tilde{\Phi}_{s}=D^{-t}\mathcal{E}_{s}(Nz)\Theta_{\bar{\mathcal{A}},\chi}(z)|_{U_{|D|}},

where

\mathcal{E}_{s}(z)=\sum_{D_{1}\cdot D_{2}}\frac{\epsilon_{D_{1}}(N)\chi_{D_{1}\cdot D_{2}}(\bar{\mathcal{A}})}{\kappa(D_{1})|D_{1}|^{s+\ell_{1}-3/2}}E_{s}^{(D_{1})}(|D_{2}|z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}e_{s}(n,y)e(nx),

with
e(x)=e^{2\pi ix}
(exactly the same Eisenstein series as in
[
22
]
, except that
k
is replaced by
k-t
, or equivalently
2k
is replaced by
\ell_{1}=2k-2t
). Formulas for the coefficients
e_{s}(n,y)
can be found in
[
22
, IV (3.2)]
(after replacing
2k
therein by
\ell_{1}
). In the above formula,
\kappa(D_{1})=1
or
i
according as
D_{1}>0
or
D_{1}<0
.

Remark 3.2
.

The character
\chi_{D_{1}\cdot D_{2}}
appearing in the above formula is the genus character associated with the partition
D=D_{1}D_{2}
. Its value at a prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}
is

\chi_{D_{1}\cdot D_{2}}(\mathfrak{p})=\begin{cases}\epsilon_{D_{1}}(N(\mathfrak{p})),&(N(\mathfrak{p}),D_{1})=1\\
\epsilon_{D_{2}}(N(\mathfrak{p})),&(N(\mathfrak{p}),D_{2})=1.\\
\end{cases}

If we let

e^{*}_{s}(n,y):=\pi^{-s}|D|^{s}\Gamma(s+2k-2t-1)e_{s}(n,y),

then the functional equation follows from the transformation property

e^{*}_{s}(n,y)=-\epsilon_{K}(N)e^{*}_{2-2k+2t-s}(n,y)

proved in
[
22
, IV §4]
.
∎

3.2.
Central derivative calculations

We assume that
K
satisfies the Heegner hypothesis (
1
), namely that every prime dividing
N
splits in
K
. This implies in particular that the sign of the functional equation of Theorem
3.1
is
-1
. Moreover, equation (
21
) implies that there exists a non-holomorphic modular form
\tilde{\Phi}
of weight
2k
and level
\Gamma_{0}(N)
such that the central value of the derivative is given by

L^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A}}(f,\chi,k+t)=\frac{2^{2k+2t+1}\pi^{k+t+1}}{(k+t-1)!\sqrt{|D|}D^{t}}(f,\tilde{\Phi})_{\Gamma_{0}(N)}.

In fact, the function is given by

(22)

\tilde{\Phi}=\frac{\sqrt{|D|}}{2\pi}N^{k-t-1}\frac{\partial}{\partial s}(D^{t}\tilde{\Phi}_{s})|_{s=1-(k-t)}.

Proposition 3.3
.

The Fourier expansion of
\tilde{\Phi}
is given by

\displaystyle\tilde{\Phi}(z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=-\infty}^{\infty}\left[-\sum_{0<n\leq\frac{m|D|}{N}}\sigma^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A}}(n)r_{\bar{\mathcal{A}},\chi}(m|D|-nN)p_{k-t-1}\left(\frac{4\pi nNy}{|D|}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{h}{u}D^{t}r_{\bar{\mathcal{A}},\chi}(m)\left(\log(y)+\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\Gamma}(k-t)+\log(N|D|)-\log(\pi)+2\frac{L^{\prime}}{L}(1,\epsilon_{K})\right)

\displaystyle\left.\qquad-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sigma_{\mathcal{A}}(n)r_{\bar{\mathcal{A}},\chi}(m|D|+nN)q_{k-t-1}\left(\frac{4\pi nNy}{|D|}\right)\right]y^{1-k+t}q^{m},

where

p_{\nu-1}(x):=\sum_{j=0}^{\nu-1}\binom{\nu-1}{j}\frac{(-x)^{j}}{j!}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad q_{\nu-1}(x):=\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{(y-1)^{\nu-1}}{y^{\nu}}e^{-xy}dy,

and
where the two first terms are zero whenever
m\leq 0
. Moreover,

\sigma_{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(n)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\mid n\\
d>0\end{subarray}}\epsilon_{\mathcal{A}}(n,d)\log\left(\frac{n}{d^{2}}\right)

and

\sigma_{\mathcal{A}}(n)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\mid n\\
d>0\end{subarray}}\epsilon_{\mathcal{A}}(n,d),

with

\epsilon_{\mathcal{A}}(n,d)=\begin{cases}0&(d,n/d,D)\neq 1\\
\epsilon_{D_{1}}(d)\epsilon_{D_{2}}(-Nn/d)\chi_{D_{1}\cdot D_{2}}(\mathcal{A})&(d,n/d,D)=1,\end{cases}

for
(d,D)=|D_{2}|
and
D_{1}D_{2}=D
.

Remark 3.4
.

The fact that
r_{\bar{\mathcal{A}},\chi}(0)=0
is proper to the
t>0
case, as
\Theta_{\mathcal{A},\chi}
is a cusp form. This is not the case when dealing with a class group character as in
[
22
]
. This simple observation leads to several simplifications when applying holomorphic projection in the next section.

Proof.

We have
D^{t}\tilde{\Phi}_{s}(z)=\mathcal{E}_{s}(Nz)\Theta_{\bar{\mathcal{A}},\chi}(z)|_{U_{|D|}}
and the Fourier expansion of
\mathcal{E}_{s}(z)
is given by
\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}e_{s}(n,y)e(nx)
. Hence, the Fourier expansion of
\mathcal{E}_{s}(Nz)\Theta_{\bar{\mathcal{A}},\chi}(z)
is

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{s}(Nz)\Theta_{\bar{\mathcal{A}},\chi}(z)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}e_{s}(n,Ny)e(nNx)\right)\left(\sum_{m\geq 0}r_{\bar{\mathcal{A}},\chi}(m)e^{-2\pi my}e(mx)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\geq 0\\
v-m\equiv 0\pmod{N}\end{subarray}}e_{s}\left(\frac{v-m}{N},Ny\right)r_{\bar{\mathcal{A}},\chi}(m)e^{-2\pi my}\right)e(vx)

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in\mathbb{Z}}A_{v}(y)e(vx),

where we made the substitution
v=nN+m
. Applying the operator
U_{|D|}
, we deduce that

\displaystyle D^{t}\tilde{\Phi}_{s}(z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in\mathbb{Z}}A_{v|D|}\left(\frac{y}{|D|}\right)e(vx)

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\geq 0\\
v-m\equiv 0\pmod{N}\end{subarray}}e_{s}\left(\frac{v|D|-m}{N},\frac{Ny}{|D|}\right)r_{\bar{\mathcal{A}},\chi}(m)e^{-2\pi m\frac{y}{|D|}}\right)e(vx)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\geq 0\\
nN+m\equiv 0\pmod{|D|}\end{subarray}}e_{s}\left(n,\frac{Ny}{|D|}\right)r_{\bar{\mathcal{A}},\chi}(m)e^{-2\pi m\frac{y}{|D|}}\right)e\left(\frac{nN+m}{|D|}x\right),

where we used the substitution
n=(v|D|-m)/N
.

We are going to focus on the terms of the Fourier expansion where
n=0
(the cases where
n>0
or
n<0
are similar to what is done in
[
22
]
), namely

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\geq 0\\
m\equiv 0\pmod{|D|}\end{subarray}}e_{s}\left(0,\frac{Ny}{|D|}\right)r_{\bar{\mathcal{A}},\chi}(m)e^{-2\pi m\frac{y}{|D|}}e\left(\frac{m}{|D|}x\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\geq 0\\
m\equiv 0\pmod{|D|}\end{subarray}}e_{s}\left(0,\frac{Ny}{|D|}\right)r_{\bar{\mathcal{A}},\chi}(m)q^{\frac{m}{|D|}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m^{\prime}\geq 0}e_{s}\left(0,\frac{Ny}{|D|}\right)r_{\bar{\mathcal{A}},\chi}(m^{\prime}|D|)q^{m^{\prime}},

where we used the substitution
m^{\prime}=m/|D|
.
We need to calculate

\sum_{m\geq 0}\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial s}e_{s}\left(0,\frac{Ny}{|D|}\right)\right|_{s=1-(k-t)}r_{\bar{\mathcal{A}},\chi}(m|D|)q^{m}.

But this was done already in
[
22
, p. 283]
(they work with the same Eisenstein series but with
k
instead of
k-t
):

\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial s}e_{s}\left(0,y\right)\right|_{s=1-(k-t)}=2L(1,\epsilon_{K})(|D|y)^{1-k+t}\left(\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\Gamma}(k-t)+\log\left(\frac{|D|^{2}y}{\pi}\right)+2\frac{L^{\prime}}{L}(1,\epsilon_{K})\right).

We have

\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial s}e_{s}\left(0,\frac{Ny}{|D|}\right)\right|_{s=1-(k-t)}=2L(1,\epsilon_{K})(Ny)^{1-k+t}\left(\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\Gamma}(k-t)+\log\left(\frac{|D|Ny}{\pi}\right)+2\frac{L^{\prime}}{L}(1,\epsilon_{K})\right)\\
=2L(1,\epsilon_{K})(Ny)^{1-k+t}\left(\log(y)+\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\Gamma}(k-t)+\log\left(N|D|\right)-\log(\pi)+2\frac{L^{\prime}}{L}(1,\epsilon_{K})\right).

We conclude that the
n=0
term of the Fourier expansion of
\tilde{\Phi}(z)
is

\sum_{m\geq 0}\frac{\sqrt{|D|}}{\pi}L(1,\epsilon_{K})r_{\bar{\mathcal{A}},\chi}(m|D|)\left(\log(y)+\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\Gamma}(k-t)+\log\left(N|D|\right)-\log(\pi)+2\frac{L^{\prime}}{L}(1,\epsilon_{K})\right)y^{1-k+t}q^{m}.

The result follows from using the formula
\frac{\sqrt{|D|}}{\pi}L(1,\epsilon_{K})=\frac{h}{u}

[
22
, p. 249]
and observing that

r_{\bar{\mathcal{A}},\chi}(m|D|)=D^{t}r_{\bar{\mathcal{A}},\chi}(m).

Note that

r_{\bar{\mathcal{A}}}(m|D|)=r_{\bar{\mathcal{A}}}(m)=r_{\mathcal{A}}(m|D|)=r_{\mathcal{A}}(m),

and
[
22
]
opted for the simpler expression
r_{\mathcal{A}}(m)
in their formula, making it easy to miss the factor of
D^{t}
in our formula (the factor of
D^{t}
is in fact missing in
[
34
, Prop. X.7]
).
∎

3.3.
Holomorphic projection

Let
g_{\mathcal{A}}=\sum_{m\geq 1}a_{m}(\mathcal{A})q^{m}\in S_{2k}^{\new}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
represent the linear functional on
S_{2k}^{\new}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
given by

f\longmapsto\frac{(2k-2)!\sqrt{|D|}D^{t}}{2^{4k-1}\pi^{2k}}L^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A}}(f,\chi,k+t).

Then
g_{\mathcal{A}}
is obtained by applying holomorphic projection lemma
[
22
, IV (5.1)]
to the function
\tilde{\Phi}
defined by (
22
).
More precisely,
g_{\mathcal{A}}
is the holomorphic projection of

\frac{(2k-2)!}{(k+t-1)!}(4\pi)^{t-k+1}\tilde{\Phi}.

Lemma 3.5
.

Assume that
0<t\leq k-1
.
The function
\tilde{\Phi}(z)=\sum_{m=-\infty}^{\infty}a_{m}(y)q^{m}
satisfies
(\tilde{\Phi}|_{2k}\alpha)(z)=O(y^{-\epsilon})
as
y\to\infty
for some
\epsilon>0
and every
\alpha\in{\rm SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

Proof.

We have the estimates
p_{\nu-1}(x)=O(x^{\nu-1})
,
q_{\nu-1}(x)=O(x^{\nu-1}e^{-x})
,
r_{\bar{\mathcal{A}},\chi}(n)=O(n^{t+1/2})
,
\sigma_{\mathcal{A}}(n)=o(n^{\delta})
and
\sigma_{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(n)=O(n^{\delta})
for all
\delta>0
. Using these estimates, and the formulas for
a_{m}(y)
derived in Proposition
3.3
, we obtain the following estimates. When
t<k-1
, we have

a_{m}(y)=\begin{cases}O(m^{\delta+k+1/2})&(m>0)\\
O(e^{-4\pi\frac{N}{|D|}y})&(m=0)\\
O(|m|^{\delta+k-t-1}e^{-4\pi|m|y})&(m<0).\end{cases}

When
t=k-1
, we have

a_{m}(y)=\begin{cases}O(m^{k-1/2}\log(y))&(m>0)\\
O(e^{-4\pi\frac{N}{|D|}y})&(m=0)\\
O(|m|^{\delta}e^{-4\pi|m|y})&(m<0).\end{cases}

In particular, for
0<t\leq k-1
, all terms in the Fourier expansion of
\tilde{\Phi}
are exponentially small as
y\to\infty
. Similar estimates hold at the other cusps.
∎

Theorem 3.6
.

If
0<t\leq k-1
, then for all
m\geq 1
such that
(m,N)=1
, we have

\displaystyle a_{m}(\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle=m^{k-t-1}\left[-\sum_{0<n\leq\frac{m|D|}{N}}\sigma^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A}}(n)r_{\bar{\mathcal{A}},\chi}(m|D|-nN)P_{k,t}\left(1-\frac{2nN}{m|D|}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{h}{u}D^{t}r_{\bar{\mathcal{A}},\chi}(m)\left(\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\Gamma}(k+t)+\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\Gamma}(k-t)+\log\left(\frac{N|D|}{4\pi^{2}m}\right)+2\frac{L^{\prime}}{L}(1,\epsilon_{K})\right)

\displaystyle\left.\qquad-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sigma_{\mathcal{A}}(n)r_{\bar{\mathcal{A}},\chi}(m|D|+nN)Q_{k,t}\left(1+\frac{2nN}{m|D|}\right)\right],

where
P_{k,t}
and
Q_{k,t}
are the Jacobi functions defined by the formulas (
11
) and (
17
) respectively.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.5
, we may apply the holomorphic projection lemma
[
22
, IV (5.1)]
to get the result.
∎

Remark 3.7
.

We have
Q_{k,t}(x)=O(x^{-k-t})
as
x\to+\infty
by Lemma
2.4
. In particular, the
n^{\text{th}}
term of the infinite sum in Theorem
3.6
is
O(n^{\delta+t+1/2-k-t})=O(n^{\delta-k+1/2})
. Since
k\geq 2
, we see that the
n^{\text{th}}
term is
O(n^{\delta-3/2})
and the sum converges. This is true even in the edge case
t=k-1
, in notable contrast with the situation in
[
22
]
where the parameters
(k,t)=(1,0)
lead to the infinite sum being divergent.

4.
Heights

Let
Y
be a smooth projective variety over a number field
F
of dimension
d=2n-1
. Assume that
Y
admits a regular model
\mathbf{Y}
that is projective and flat over
\spec\mathcal{O}_{F}
. Let
Z_{1}
and
Z_{2}
be algebraic cycles on
Y
of codimension
n
. Assume the following conditions:

(a)

Z_{1}
has an integral model
\mathbf{Z}_{1}
in
\mathbf{Y}
with the property that
\mathbf{Z}_{1}
has zero intersection with any algebraic cycle of codimension
n
on
\mathbf{Y}
supported on the special fibers.

(b)

Z_{2}
admits an integral model
\mathbf{Z}_{2}
in
\mathbf{Y}
.

(c)

Z_{1}
is null-homologous, i.e., its class in
H^{2n}(Y(\mathbb{C}),\mathbb{C})
is zero.

Condition
(c)
implies that there is a Green’s current
g_{1}
on
Y(\mathbb{C})
such that
\frac{\partial\bar{\partial}}{\pi i}g_{1}=\delta_{Z_{1}}
, where
\delta_{Z_{1}}
is the current associated to
Z_{1}
which maps any real valued differential
\eta\in A^{n,n}(Y_{\mathbb{R}})
to
\int_{Z_{1,\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{C})}\eta
.

Under the above assumptions, the height pairing of
Z_{1}
and
Z_{2}
is defined as

\langle Z_{1},Z_{2}\rangle^{\GS}:=(-1)^{n}(\mathbf{Z}_{1},g_{1})\cdot(\mathbf{Z}_{2},g_{2}),

where
g_{2}
is any Green’s current for
Z_{2,\mathbb{C}}
.
The intersection on the right hand side is the arithmetic intersection pairing of Gillet–Soulé

\widehat{\CH}^{n}(\mathbf{Y})_{\mathbb{R}}\times\widehat{\CH}^{n}(\mathbf{Y})_{\mathbb{R}}{\longrightarrow\,}\mathbb{R},

defined for arithmetic cycles
\widehat{T}_{i}=(\mathbf{T}_{i},\alpha_{i})
,
i=1,2
, that are irreducible and intersect properly, by

\widehat{T}_{1}\cdot\widehat{T}_{2}:=\log(|\Gamma(\mathbf{T}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{T}_{2},\mathcal{O})|)+\int_{\mathbf{T}_{2,\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{C})}\alpha_{1}+\int_{\mathbf{Y}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{C})}\alpha_{2}\beta_{1},

where
\beta_{1}=\delta_{T_{1}}-\frac{\partial\bar{\partial}}{\pi i}\alpha_{1}

[
17
, (4.2.2.2)]
. Because
\mathbf{Y}
is regular, there is a moving lemma for arithmetic Chow groups, and the arithmetic intersection pairing is defined for all
\widehat{T}_{1}
and
\widehat{T}_{2}

[
41
, p. 104]
.

4.1.
Local height decomposition

The arithmetic intersection pairing admits a decomposition into a sum of local intersections for all places
v
of
F
:

\widehat{T}_{1}\cdot\widehat{T}_{2}=\sum_{v}(\mathbf{T}_{1,v}\cdot\mathbf{T}_{2,v})\epsilon_{v},

where
\epsilon_{v}=\log q_{v}
with
q_{v}=|\mathcal{O}_{F}/\mathfrak{p}|
if
v
is a non-archimedean place corresponding to a prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}
, and
\epsilon_{v}=2
if
v
is a complex archimedean place.
It follows that the height pairing also admits a decomposition into local components

\langle Z_{1},Z_{2}\rangle^{\GS}=\sum_{v}\langle Z_{1},Z_{2}\rangle_{v}\epsilon_{v}.

These local intersections are only defined when
\mathbf{Z}_{1}
and
\mathbf{Z}_{2}
do not intersect in the generic fiber
Y=\mathbf{Y}_{F}
, and they depend on the representatives of the cycles
Z_{1}
and
Z_{2}
.

4.2.
Local heights in cases of improper intersection

For the explicit cycles that we work with, we do not wish to use a moving lemma and it becomes crucial to define local heights even when the cycles do intersect in the generic fiber. Below we give such a local decomposition when
Y
is a compactification of an abelian scheme
f\colon\mathcal{A}\to C
of relative dimension
2n-2
over a smooth curve
C
, and when the cycles
Z_{1}
and
Z_{2}
are supported on sub-abelian varieties in fibers of
f
.
5
5

5

We prove this below under an additional technical assumption but we suspect the method works in general.
This is done by combining a result of Zhang
[
41
]
with Brylinski’s formalism for heights on local systems over curves
[
10
]
.

Now suppose
Z_{1}
and
Z_{2}
are such cycles (of codimension
n
on
\mathcal{A}
) supported on the fiber of
f
above a single point
x\in C
.

4.2.1.
Archimedean local height

Let
v
be an archimedean place of
F
. A choice of polarization on
\mathcal{A}
makes
V=R^{2n-2}f_{*}\mathbb{Z}(n-1)
a weight 0 polarized variation of Hodge structures (PVHS) on
C_{v}=C\times_{v}\mathbb{C}
with associated vector bundle
\mathcal{V}
(see Definition
6.3
for the precise definition). Brylinski
[
10
]
attaches to such a PVHS a Green’s kernel and defines a local height pairing on Hodge classes. Let us write
\bar{C}
for the compactification of
C
. There is a canonical way to extend
\mathcal{V}
to a vector bundle
\bar{\mathcal{V}}
on
\bar{C}_{v}
due to Deligne (characterized by certain properties)
[
10
, §1 p. 4]
. Let us write
\bar{\mathcal{V}}_{0,\mathbb{R}}
for the
C^{\infty}
vector bundle of sections of
\bar{\mathcal{V}}
which are real and of type
(0,0)
. Also write
\pr_{1}
and
\pr_{2}
for the projections
\bar{C}\times\bar{C}{\longrightarrow\,}\bar{C}
.

Proposition 4.1

(Proposition 2.9
[
10
]
)
.

If
V
has no non-zero global section, there exists a unique
C^{\infty}
-section
G
of
\mathrm{Hom}(\pr_{1}^{-1}\bar{\mathcal{V}}_{0,\mathbb{R}},\pr_{2}^{-1}\bar{\mathcal{V}}_{0,\mathbb{R}})
over the complement of the diagonal in
\bar{C}_{v}\times\bar{C}_{v}
, such that

(1)

\square_{2}G=0
, where
\square_{2}
is the Laplacian associated with
\mathcal{V}
in the second variable;

(2)

G(x,y)-\log|z(x)-z(y)|
is bounded near any point
(a,a)\in\bar{C}_{v}\times\bar{C}_{v}
, if
z
is a local coordinate on
\bar{C}_{v}
near
a
.

The
C^{\infty}
-section
G
, called the Green’s kernel attached to
V
, can be used to define the Brylinski pairing. If
x\neq y\in C_{v}(\mathbb{C})
and
v_{x}^{1}
and
v_{y}^{2}
are type
(0,0)
classes supported in the fibers of
V
above
x
and
y
respectively, Brylinski defines
[
10
, Prop. 2.11]

(23)

\left\langle v^{1}_{x},v^{2}_{y}\right\rangle^{\mathrm{Br}}:=\langle G(x,y)v^{1}_{x},v^{2}_{y}\rangle_{y},

where
\langle\,,\,\rangle_{y}
denotes the Hermitian pairing induced by the polarization on the fiber of
V
above
y
. This extends by linearity to a pairing on fibral classes supported on disjoints sets of fibers.
If
v_{x}^{1}
and
v_{y}^{2}
are cycle classes of homologically trivial cycles
Z
and
Z^{\prime}
supported on the fibers above
x
and
y
(distinct points) respectively, then
[
10
, Appendix]

(24)

\left\langle v^{1}_{x},v^{2}_{y}\right\rangle^{\mathrm{Br}}=\langle Z,Z^{\prime}\rangle_{v}.

(See also
[
30
]
for various definitions of archimedean local height pairing and comparisons between them.)

Recall that
Z_{1}
and
Z_{2}
are supported on the fiber of
f
above a single point
x\in C
. Their images, denoted
v^{1}_{x}
and
v^{2}_{x}
respectively, under the cycle class map relative to the fiber
f^{-1}(x)
(after base-change to
\mathbb{C}
via
v
) lie in the fiber of
V
above
x
. Let
\eta
be a
(n-1,n-1)
-form on
Y_{v}(\mathbb{C})
such that for all points
y
close enough to
x
, the restriction
\eta_{y}
to the fiber above
y
satisfies
\partial\eta_{y}=0=\bar{\partial}\eta_{y}
, and moreover the restriction
\eta_{x}
recovers the cycle class of
v^{2}_{x}
, thought of as a differential form. For
y\in C_{v}(\mathbb{C})
close to
X
, let
v^{2}_{y}\in V_{y}
correspond to
\eta_{y}
.

Let
t
be a uniformizer on
C_{v}(\mathbb{C})
at
x
. We let

(25)

\langle Z_{1},Z_{2}\rangle_{v}=\lim_{y\to x}\left(\langle G(x,y)(v^{1}_{x},v^{2}_{y})\rangle_{y}+(-1)^{n}(Z_{1}\cdot Z_{2})_{x}\log|t(y)|_{v}\right),

where
(Z_{1}\cdot Z_{2})_{x}
is the usual (geometric) intersection pairing in the fiber
f^{-1}(x)
.

4.2.2.
Non-archimedean local height

If
v
is a non-archimedean place of
F
, we define

\langle Z_{1},Z_{2}\rangle_{v}=(Z_{1}\cdot Z_{2})_{x}\ord_{v}\,d_{x}t,

where
\ord_{v}\,d_{x}t
is defined as in
[
41
, p. 106]
.

4.2.3.
Global conjecture

Conjecture 4.2
.

Suppose
Z_{1}
and
Z_{2}
are supported on a single common fiber of
f
. Then

\langle Z_{1},Z_{2}\rangle^{\GS}=\sum_{v}\langle Z_{1},Z_{2}\rangle_{v},

where the local pairings are defined as above.

For our purposes it will be enough to prove the following special case.

Theorem 4.3
.

With hypotheses and notation as above, suppose further that there exists a dense set of points
W\subset U
such that the class
v_{y}^{2}
of (
25
) is the class of an algebraic cycle
Z_{y}
in
f^{-1}(y)
for all
y\in W
. Then Conjecture
4.2
holds.

Proof.

This follows from the results of Zhang and Brylinski mentioned earlier. Zhang’s
[
41
, Conj. 1.2.1 & Thm. 1.2.2]
says that Conjecture
4.2
is true with archimedean local heights given by

(-1)^{n}\lim_{y\to x}\left(\int_{\mathcal{A}_{y}}g_{1}\eta+(Z_{1}\cdot Z_{2})_{x}\log|t(y)|_{v}\right),

where
g_{1}
is the Green’s current for
Z_{1}
. In the fiber above some
y\in W
, the integral
(-1)^{n}\int_{\mathcal{A}_{y}}g_{1}\eta
is by definition the local Gillet–Soulé height
\langle Z_{1},Z_{y}\rangle_{v}
, which is in turn equal to
\langle v_{x}^{1},v_{y}^{2}\rangle^{\mathrm{Br}}
by (
24
). Applying (
23
), we obtain

(-1)^{n}\int_{\mathcal{A}_{y}}g_{1}\eta=\langle G(x,y)v_{x}^{1},v_{y}^{2}\rangle_{y},\qquad\forall y\in W.

The left hand side and the right hand side can both be viewed as function on
U\setminus\{x\}
. As such, they are continuous, and since they agree on a dense set they must be equal. We conclude that Conjecture
4.2
is true with archimedean local heights given by (
25
).
∎

5.
Generalized Heegner cycles

Let
K/\mathbb{Q}
be an imaginary quadratic field, and let
H
be its Hilbert class field. Let
(A,\iota)
be an elliptic curve
A/H
with complex multiplication
\iota\colon\mathcal{O}_{K}\hookrightarrow\End(A)
by
\mathcal{O}_{K}
.

5.1.
Generalized Kuga–Sato varieties

For any
M\geq 3
, let
X(M)
be the modular curve (over
H
) parameterizing pairs
(E,(\alpha,\beta))
, where
E
is an elliptic curve and
(\alpha,\beta)
is a basis for
E[M]
. Note that
X(M)
is geometrically disconnected since we impose no condition on the Weil pairing
\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle
.
For
k\geq 1
, let
W_{2k-2}
be the smooth and projective Kuga–Sato
(2k-1)
-fold over
X(M)
, which is birational to the
(2k-2)
-th fiber power
\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{(2k-2)}
of the universal generalized elliptic curve over
X(M)

[
41
, §2]
.

For
\ell\geq 0
, we consider the variety

X=X_{M,k,t}:=W_{2k-2}\times_{H}A^{\ell},

which we view as fibered over
X(M)
. It is smooth and projective over
H
, of dimension
2k+\ell-1
, birational to
\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{(2k-2)}\times_{H}A^{\ell}
.

5.2.
Cycles

Let
F
be a finite extension of
H
and
Q\in X(M)(F)
a point corresponding to a full level
M
-structure
B=(\alpha,\beta)
of
A
.
Any isogeny
\varphi\colon A_{F}\to A^{\prime}
over
F
of degree prime to
M
gives another point
Q_{\varphi}:=(A^{\prime},\varphi(B))
of
X(M)(F)
.
The fiber of
\bar{\mathcal{E}}{\longrightarrow\,}X(M)
above
Q_{\varphi}
is isomorphic to
A^{\prime}
. The fiber
X_{Q_{\varphi}}
of
X{\longrightarrow\,}X(M)
over
Q_{\varphi}
is isomorphic to
(A^{\prime})^{2k-2}\times A_{F}^{\ell}
.

For a homomorphism
\varphi\in\Hom(A_{1},A_{2})
between elliptic curves, we denote by
\Gamma_{\varphi}\subset A_{1}\times A_{2}
its graph, and by
\Gamma^{T}_{\varphi}\subset A_{2}\times A_{1}
the transpose of
\Gamma_{\varphi}
. We choose a square root
\sqrt{D}\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
and view
\sqrt{D}
as an element of
\End(A)
if no confusion can arise, e.g., in the notation
\Gamma_{\sqrt{D}}\subset A\times A
.

Assume from now on that
\ell=2t
is even and
0<t\leq k-1
. For
\varphi\colon A_{F}\to A^{\prime}
as above, define

Y_{\varphi}:=\Gamma_{\sqrt{D}}^{k-1-t}\times(\Gamma_{\varphi}^{T})^{2t}\subset(A^{\prime}\times A^{\prime})^{k-1-t}\times(A^{\prime}\times A_{F})^{2t}\simeq X_{Q_{\varphi}}\subset X_{F}.

For each ideal
\mathfrak{a}\trianglelefteq\mathcal{O}_{K}
coprime to
M
, we have an isogeny
\phi_{\mathfrak{a}}:A{\longrightarrow\,}A/A[\mathfrak{a}]=:A^{\mathfrak{a}}
. We write
Q^{\mathfrak{a}}:=Q_{\phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}
and
Y^{\mathfrak{a}}:=Y_{\phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}
.
When
\mathfrak{a}=\mathcal{O}_{K}
, we recover the point
Q=Q^{\mathcal{O}_{K}}=(\alpha,\beta)\in X(M)(F)
. We write
Y^{\mathfrak{a}}=Y^{\mathfrak{a}}_{Q}
if we want to emphasize the dependence on the initial choice of point
Q
.

Let
{\rm Corr}^{0}(X,X)_{K}
be the ring of algebraic correspondences modulo rational equivalence on
X
with coefficients in
K
, as in
[
4
, p. 755]
.
We define the idempotent

\epsilon:=\epsilon_{W}\otimes\epsilon_{2t}\in{\rm Corr}^{0}(X,X)_{K},

where
\epsilon_{W}
is the Deligne–Scholl
[
33
]
projector on
W_{2k-2}
(i.e., the Chow motive of weight
2k
cusp forms) and, for any
m\geq 1
,
\epsilon_{m}
is the idempotent

\epsilon_{m}:=\left(\frac{\sqrt{D}+[\sqrt{D}]}{2\sqrt{D}}\right)^{\otimes m}\circ\left(\frac{1-[-1]}{2}\right)^{\otimes m}\in{\rm Corr}^{0}(A^{m},A^{m})_{K}.

Define also

\bar{\epsilon}_{m}:=\left(\frac{\sqrt{D}-[\sqrt{D}]}{2\sqrt{D}}\right)^{\otimes m}\circ\left(\frac{1-[-1]}{2}\right)^{\otimes m}\in{\rm Corr}^{0}(A^{m},A^{m})_{K},

and

(26)

\kappa_{m}=\epsilon_{m}+\bar{\epsilon}_{m}.

Define

\bar{\epsilon}:=\epsilon_{W}\otimes\bar{\epsilon}_{2t}\in{\rm Corr}^{0}(X,X)_{K},

\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon_{W}\otimes\kappa_{2t}\in{\rm Corr}^{0}(X,X)_{\mathbb{Q}}.

For any Weil cohomology theory
H^{*}(-)
, we have
[
3
, Prop. 2.4]

\displaystyle\epsilon_{W}H^{*}(W_{2k-2})

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{W}H^{2k-1}(W_{2k-2})

\displaystyle\kappa_{2t}H^{*}(A^{2t})

\displaystyle=\kappa_{2t}H^{2t}(A^{2t}),

and so
\epsilon^{\prime}H^{*}(X)=\epsilon^{\prime}H^{2k+2t-1}(X)
.

Lemma 5.1
.

For all
B=(\alpha,\beta)
and
\varphi\colon A_{F}\to A^{\prime}
as above, the cycles
\epsilon Y_{\varphi}
and
\bar{\epsilon}Y_{\varphi}
are null-homologous. In other words,

\epsilon Y_{\varphi}\in\CH^{k+t}(X_{F})_{0,K}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\bar{\epsilon}Y_{\varphi}\in\CH^{k+t}(X_{F})_{0,K},

where
\CH^{j}(X_{F})_{0,K}
denotes the kernel of the cycle class map
\CH^{j}(X_{F})_{K}\to H^{2j}(X)(j)\otimes K
.

Proof.

This follows from the fact that
\epsilon^{\prime}
(and hence
\epsilon
and
\bar{\epsilon}
) annihilates even degree cohomology.
∎

5.3.
Generalized Heegner cycles

Let
N
be a divisor of
M
, and let
\pi_{M,N}\colon X(M)\to X_{0}(N)
be the natural morphism of curves over
H
given by
(E,\alpha,\beta)\mapsto(E,\langle\frac{M}{N}\alpha\rangle)
.

We assume from now on the Heegner hypothesis (
1
): every prime dividing
N
splits in
K
. This guarantees that there exists an ideal
\mathfrak{n}\trianglelefteq\mathcal{O}_{K}
such that
\mathcal{O}_{K}/\mathfrak{n}\simeq\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}
, and hence
A[N]\simeq A[\mathfrak{n}]\oplus A[\overline{\mathfrak{n}}]
. The pair
(A,A[\mathfrak{n}])
corresponds to a Heegner point
P_{\mathfrak{n}}\in X_{0}(N)(H)

[
20
]
. Let
F/H
be a finite extension splitting the finite
H
-scheme
\pi^{-1}_{M,N}(P_{\mathfrak{n}})
. For each
\mathfrak{a}\trianglelefteq\mathcal{O}_{K}
coprime to
M
, define

(27)

Z^{\mathfrak{a}}_{\mathfrak{n}}=\sum_{Q\in\pi_{M,N}^{-1}(P_{\mathfrak{n}})(F)}\epsilon Y_{Q}^{\mathfrak{a}}\in\CH^{k+t}(X)_{0,K}

(28)

\bar{Z}^{\mathfrak{a}}_{\mathfrak{n}}=\sum_{Q\in\pi_{M,N}^{-1}(P_{\mathfrak{n}})(F)}\bar{\epsilon}Y_{Q}^{\mathfrak{a}}\in\CH^{k+t}(X)_{0,K},

which a priori only live in
\CH^{k+t}(X_{F})_{K}
, but clearly descend to
\CH^{k+t}(X)_{0,K}
.

Let
\chi
be an unramified Hecke character of
K
of type
(2t,0)
. For each ideal class
\mathcal{A}=[\mathfrak{a}]
, we choose a representative
\mathfrak{a}
of norm prime to
M
and define

Z_{\mathcal{A}}:=\chi(\mathfrak{a})^{-1}Z_{\mathfrak{n}}^{\mathfrak{a}}\in\CH^{k+t}(X)_{0,K(\chi)},

\bar{Z}_{\mathcal{A}}:=\chi(\bar{\mathfrak{a}})^{-1}\bar{Z}_{\mathfrak{n}}^{\mathfrak{a}}\in\CH^{k+t}(X)_{0,K(\chi)}.

Assuming the conjectural injectivity of Abel–Jacobi maps, the cycles
Z_{\mathcal{A}}
and
\bar{Z}_{\mathcal{A}}
do not depend on the choice of representative ideal
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathcal{A}
(see
[
35
, Prop. 4.6]
).
Finally, we define

(29)

\Delta_{\chi}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\deg(\pi_{M,N})}}\sum_{\mathcal{A}}(Z_{\mathcal{A}}+\bar{Z}_{\bar{\mathcal{A}}})\in\CH^{k+t}(X)_{0,\mathbb{C}}.

Remark 5.2
.

The cycle
\Delta_{\chi}
depends on the choice of
\mathfrak{n}
, but its Beilinson–Bloch height
is independent of this choice, since the Atkin–Lehner involutions on
X_{0}(N)
act transitively on the points
P_{\mathfrak{n}}
. Likewise the height of
\Delta_{\chi}

is independent of
M
due to the square root normalization factor.

5.4.
Integral models

In order to compute heights we must use integral models of generalized Kuga–Sato variety and generalized Heegner cycles, as discussed in Section
4
.

Suppose
M
is a multiple of
N
such that
M=N_{1}N_{2}
with
(N_{1},N_{2})=1
and
N_{i}\geq 3
. We will compute heights over a finite extension
F/H
which is unramified at
M
and with the property that
A
has everywhere good reduction over
F
. These two conditions are compatible: since
N
is prime to
D=\Disc(K)
, we can always choose
A
and
M
so that
A
has good reduction at all primes dividing
M
, and we may then take
F
to be the minimal number field over which
A
attains good reduction. We may and do moreover assume that all primes dividing
M
split in
K
. We write
X
,
X(M)
, et cetera, instead of
X_{F},X(M)_{F}
, etc.

Let
\mathbf{W}\to\mathcal{O}_{F}
be the proper regular model for
W_{2k-2}
constructed in
[
41
, §2.2]
, and let
\mathbf{A}
be the Néron model for
A
over
\mathcal{O}_{F}
. Since
A/F
has everywhere good reduction,
\mathbf{A}\to\mathcal{O}_{F}
is smooth and we conclude:

Proposition 5.3
.

\mathbf{X}:=\mathbf{W}\times_{\mathcal{O}_{F}}\mathbf{A}^{\ell}
is a proper regular model for
X
over
\mathcal{O}_{F}
.

As in
[
41
, 3.1]
, we may extend the cycles
Z_{\mathfrak{n}}^{\mathfrak{a}}
and
\bar{Z}_{\mathfrak{n}}^{\mathfrak{a}}
to cycles on
\mathbf{X}
so that the height pairing
\langle\Delta_{\chi},\Delta_{\chi}\rangle^{\GS}_{F}
is well-defined (i.e., conditions (a)-(c) of Section
4
are satisfied). Just as in
[
41
]
, we are implicitly making use of the fact that in characteristic
p\mid M
, generalized Heegner cycles live in fibers above ordinary (and hence smooth!) points of
X(M)_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
, so that the Zariski closure of the cycles in the generic fiber automatically satisfy condition
(a)
.

5.5.
Hecke operators

The usual Hecke operators on higher weight modular forms come from Hecke correspondence on
W_{2k-2}
, and these extend to
\mathbf{W}
as in
[
41
, 2.2]
. We define correspondences
T_{m}
on
X
(and similarly
\mathbf{X}
) by pullback from
W_{2k-2}
(resp.
\mathbf{W}
).

6.
Variations of Hodge structures

In order to use Brylinski’s formalism to compute heights of generalized Heegner cycles, we need to construct the Green’s kernel of Proposition
4.1
associated to a certain polarized variation of Hodge structures (PVHS) over
X(M)
. In this section we define the relevant PVHS, and in the next section we compute the associated Laplacian.

6.1.
Polarized variations of Hodge structures

Let
T
be a subring of
\mathbb{R}
, and let
H
be a
T
-Hodge structure of weight
m
.

Definition 6.1
.

A polarization on
H
is a
(-1)^{m}
-symmetric pairing
(\;,\;)\colon H\times H{\longrightarrow\,}T
, whose base-change
H_{\mathbb{C}}\times H_{\mathbb{C}}{\longrightarrow\,}\mathbb{C}
satisfies
(H_{\mathbb{C}}^{p,q},H_{\mathbb{C}}^{p^{\prime},q^{\prime}})=0
unless
(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})=(q,p)
, as well as
i^{p-q}(v,\bar{v})>0
for all
0\neq v\in H_{\mathbb{C}}^{p,q}
.

Remark 6.2
.

The Hermitian form
\langle v,w\rangle:=i^{p-q}(v,\bar{w})
on
H_{\mathbb{C}}^{p,q}
is positive-definite. Moreover, the orthogonal complement of
\Fil^{p}H_{\mathbb{C}}
with respect to
(\;,\;)
is
\Fil^{m-p+1}H_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Let
X
be a complex manifold and
V
a local system of finitely generated
T
-modules over
X
. Let
V_{\mathbb{C}}=V\otimes_{T}\mathbb{C}
be the local system of finite dimensional complex vector spaces over
X
, and let
\mathcal{V}:=\mathcal{O}_{X}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V_{\mathbb{C}}
be the corresponding holomorphic vector bundle over
X
with integrable connection
\nabla:=\partial\otimes 1
.

Definition 6.3
.

A
T
-PVHS with underlying local system
V
of weight
m
is the data of:
6
6

6

Technically speaking, the PVHS in Definition
6.3
is
(V,\Fil^{\bullet}\mathcal{V})
, but in practice we will simply call
\mathcal{V}
the PVHS, when no confusion can arise.

•

a finite decreasing filtration by holomorphic subbundles
\Fil^{p+1}\mathcal{V}\subset\Fil^{p}\mathcal{V}
, each locally a direct factor in
\mathcal{V}
;

•

a flat bilinear pairing
(\>,\>)\colon V\times V{\longrightarrow\,}T(-m)
;

with the following properties:

(i)

For each
x\in X
, the induced filtration
\Fil^{p}_{x}:=\Fil^{p}\mathcal{V}_{x}
on the fiber
\mathcal{V}_{x}
is the Hodge filtration of a pure Hodge structure of weight
m
;

(ii)

\nabla(\Fil^{p}\mathcal{V})\subset\Omega^{1}_{X}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\Fil^{p-1}\mathcal{V}
(Griffiths transversality);

(iii)

For each
x\in X
,
(2\pi i)^{m}(\>,\>)_{x}
gives a polarization on
\mathcal{V}_{x}
in the sense of Definition
6.1
.

Remark 6.4
.

Let
\mathcal{V}^{\infty}:=\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{X}\otimes\mathcal{V}
be the associated
C^{\infty}
-bundle with integrable connection
D:=d\otimes\id+\id\otimes\nabla
. The bilinear pairing
(\>,\>)
of Definition
6.3
(2) induces an
\mathcal{O}_{X}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{O}_{X}}
-linear pairing
(\>,\>)\colon\mathcal{V}^{\infty}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}_{X}^{\infty}}\overline{\mathcal{V}^{\infty}}{\longrightarrow\,}\mathcal{C}_{X}^{\infty}
. The flatness in Definition
6.3
(2) means that

d(u,\bar{v})=(Du,\overline{v})+(u,\overline{Dv}).

6.2.
PVHS over modular curves

Let
M\geq 3
be an integer. Let
\bar{C}=X(M)_{\mathbb{C}}
denote the compact modular curve of full level
M
structure, base-changed to
\mathbb{C}
. The open modular curve
Y(M)_{\mathbb{C}}
will be denoted by
C
, and we let
\pi=\pi_{\mathcal{E}}\colon\mathcal{E}{\longrightarrow\,}C
denote the corresponding universal elliptic curve over
C
. Recall that
C(\mathbb{C})
is the disjoint union of
\varphi(M)
copies of
\Gamma(M)\backslash\mathcal{H}
.

Consider the relative de Rham cohomology sheaf on
C

\mathcal{L}:=\mathbb{R}^{1}\pi_{*}(0{\longrightarrow\,}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}}{\longrightarrow\,}\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{E}/C}{\longrightarrow\,}0)=\mathbb{H}^{1}_{\dR}(\mathcal{E}/C).

It is a vector bundle of rank
2
over
C
, whose fibers are given by
\mathcal{L}_{Q}=H^{1}_{\dR}(E_{z})
, where
Q=\Gamma(M)z\in C(\mathbb{C})
and
E_{z}:=\pi^{-1}(z)=\mathbb{C}/\langle 1,z\rangle
. This vector bundle comes equipped with an integrable connection

\nabla\colon\mathcal{L}{\longrightarrow\,}\Omega^{1}_{C}\otimes\mathcal{L}

called the Gauss–Manin connection, as well as a canonical pairing

(\>,\>)\colon\mathcal{L}\times\mathcal{L}{\longrightarrow\,}\mathcal{O}_{C},

given on the fiber at
Q=\Gamma(M)z\in C(\mathbb{C})
by

(30)

(\>,\>)_{z}\colon H^{1}_{\dR}(E_{z})\times H^{1}_{\dR}(E_{z}){\longrightarrow\,}\mathbb{C},\qquad(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})_{z}=\int_{E_{z}}\omega_{1}\wedge\omega_{2}.

Remark 6.5
.

Together with the Hodge filtration given by the line bundle
\Fil^{1}\mathcal{L}=\underline{\omega}:=\pi_{*}\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{E}/C}
,
the pairing

(\>,\>)_{-1}\colon\mathcal{L}\times\mathcal{L}{\longrightarrow\,}\mathcal{O}_{C}(-1)

given on the fiber at
Q=\Gamma(M)z\in C(\mathbb{C})
by

(31)

(\>,\>)_{-1,z}\colon H^{1}_{\dR}(E_{z})\times H^{1}_{\dR}(E_{z}){\longrightarrow\,}\mathbb{C},\qquad(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})_{-1,z}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{E_{z}}\omega_{1}\wedge\omega_{2}

makes
\mathcal{L}
into a PVHS of weight
1
with underlying local system
R^{1}\pi_{*}\mathbb{Z}
, in the sense of Definition
6.3
.

Remark 6.6
.

The vector bundle
\mathcal{L}
admits a canonical extension to a vector bundle
\bar{\mathcal{L}}
over
\bar{C}
(see
[
3
, p. 1043-1044]
or
[
10
, p. 4]
). The Gauss–Manin connection extends to a connection with log poles

\nabla\colon\bar{\mathcal{L}}{\longrightarrow\,}\Omega^{1}_{\bar{C}}(\log Z)\otimes\bar{\mathcal{L}},

where
Z
denotes the cuspidal divisor
\bar{C}\setminus C
. The above pairing also admits an extension. Because the cycles defined in Section
5
live in non-cuspidal fibers of Kuga–Sato varieties, we will not have any need for the defining properties of these extensions.

Given
n\geq 1
, we define vector bundles
\mathcal{L}_{n}:=\sym^{n}\mathcal{L}
and write
\nabla_{n}\colon\mathcal{L}_{n}{\longrightarrow\,}\Omega^{1}_{C}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{n}
for the induced connections and

(\>,\>)_{n}\colon\mathcal{L}_{n}\times\mathcal{L}_{n}{\longrightarrow\,}\mathcal{O}_{C},

for the induced pairing given by

(x_{1}\ldots x_{n},y_{1}\ldots y_{n})_{n}:=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}(x_{1},y_{\sigma(1)})\ldots(x_{n},y_{\sigma(n)}).

Remark 6.7
.

The normalized pairing

(\>,\>)_{-n}:=(2\pi i)^{-n}(\>,\>)_{n}\colon\mathcal{L}_{n}\times\mathcal{L}_{n}{\longrightarrow\,}\mathcal{O}_{C}(-n)

makes
\mathcal{L}_{n}
into a PVHS of weight
n
with underlying local system
\sym^{n}R^{1}\pi_{*}\mathbb{Z}
, in the sense of Definition
6.3
.

Furthermore, for
m\geq 0
, we define vector bundles

\mathcal{L}_{n,m}:=\mathcal{L}_{n}\otimes\kappa_{m}H^{m}(A^{m}_{\mathbb{C}}),

with
\kappa_{m}
defined by (
26
).
We naturally get a connection

\nabla_{n,m}\colon\mathcal{L}_{n,m}{\longrightarrow\,}\Omega^{1}_{C}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{n,m}

and a pairing

(\>,\>)_{n,m}\colon\mathcal{L}_{n,m}\times\mathcal{L}_{n,m}{\longrightarrow\,}\mathcal{O}_{C},

using the canonical pairing (
30
) on
H^{1}_{\dR}(A_{\mathbb{C}})
.

Remark 6.8
.

The normalized pairing

(\>,\>)_{-n,-m}:=(2\pi i)^{-(n+m)}(\>,\>)_{n,m}\colon\mathcal{L}_{n,m}\times\mathcal{L}_{n,m}{\longrightarrow\,}\mathcal{O}_{C}(-(n+m))

makes
\mathcal{L}_{n,m}
into a PVHS of weight
n+m
with underlying local system
\sym^{n}R^{1}\pi_{*}\mathbb{Z}\otimes\kappa_{m}H^{m}_{B}(A^{m},\mathbb{Z})
.

Consider the local systems

\mathbb{L}^{B}:=R^{1}\pi_{*}\mathbb{Z},\qquad\mathbb{L}^{B}_{n}:=\sym^{n}\bL^{B},\qquad\mathbb{L}^{B}_{n,m}:=\bL^{B}_{n}\otimes\kappa_{m}H^{m}_{B}(A^{m},\mathbb{Z}),

with fibers at
Q=\Gamma(M)z\in C(\mathbb{C})
given respectively by the Betti cohomology groups

\mathbb{L}^{B}(Q):=H^{1}_{B}(E_{z},\mathbb{Z}),\;\mathbb{L}^{B}_{n}(Q):=\sym^{n}H^{1}_{B}(E_{z},\mathbb{Z}),\;\mathbb{L}^{B}_{n,m}(Q):=\sym^{n}H^{1}_{B}(E_{z},\mathbb{Z})\otimes\kappa_{m}H^{m}_{B}(A^{m},\mathbb{Z}).

The associated complex local systems

\mathbb{L}:=\mathbb{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{L}^{B},\qquad\mathbb{L}_{n}=\mathbb{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{L}^{B}_{n},\qquad\mathbb{L}_{n,m}:=\mathbb{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{L}^{B}_{n,m}

are the sheaves of horizontal sections of
(\mathcal{L},\nabla),(\mathcal{L}_{n},\nabla_{n})
, and
(\mathcal{L}_{n,m},\nabla_{n,m})
over
C(\mathbb{C})
(in the complex topology). In other words, we have

\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{O}_{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\bL,\qquad\mathcal{L}_{n}=\mathcal{O}_{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\bL_{n},\qquad\mathcal{L}_{n,m}=\mathcal{O}_{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\bL_{n,m}.

Later, we will be interested in the case were
n=2k-2
and
m=2t
, and we will consider the vector bundle

(32)

\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{W}_{k,t}:=\mathcal{L}_{2k-2,2t}(k+t-1)=\mathcal{L}_{2k-2}(k-1)\otimes\kappa_{2t}H^{2t}_{\dR}(A_{\mathbb{C}}^{2t})(t).

The pairing

(\>,\>)_{k,t}^{0}:=(\>,\>)_{-2(k-1),-2t}\colon\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{W}{\longrightarrow\,}\mathcal{O}_{C}

makes
\mathcal{W}
into a PVHS of weight
0
, in the sense of Definition
6.3
, with underlying local system

W=W_{k,t}:=(\sym^{2k-2}R^{1}\pi_{*}\mathbb{Z})(k-1)\otimes\kappa_{2t}H_{B}^{2t}(A^{2t},\mathbb{Z})(t).

In view of Remark
6.6
, the PVHS
\mathcal{W}
admits a canonical extension
\bar{\mathcal{W}}
to a vector bundle on
\bar{C}
equipped with a connection with log poles.

6.3.
PVHS over the upper-half plane

We use the following conventions (see also Section
1.6
): a point of the upper-half plane
\mathcal{H}
will be denoted by
z=x+iy
, while the notation
\tau\in\mathcal{H}
will be reserved for imaginary quadratic points. The complex coordinate on the elliptic curve
\mathbb{C}/\langle 1,z\rangle
will be denoted by
w
, and the same goes for a CM elliptic curve
\mathbb{C}/\langle 1,\tau\rangle
.

Consider the projection map

\pr\colon\bigsqcup\mathcal{H}{\longrightarrow\,}\bigsqcup\Gamma(M)\backslash\mathcal{H}=C(\mathbb{C}),

with the disjoint union indexed by the primitive
M^{\text{th}}
roots of unity.
Using this projection, we pull all the previously defined structures back to obtain vector bundles and local systems over
\bigsqcup\mathcal{H}
:

\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{n}:=\pr^{*}(\mathcal{L}_{n}),\qquad\tilde{\mathbb{L}}_{n}:=\pr^{*}(\mathbb{L}_{n}),\qquad\text{etc...}

Let
W_{2k-2}^{0}:=W_{2k-2}\times_{\bar{C}}C=(\mathcal{E})^{2k-2}
and
X^{0}:=X\times_{\bar{C}}C=(\mathcal{E})^{2k-2}\times_{H}A^{2t}
. Denote by
\tcE:=\pr^{*}(\mathcal{E})
the pull-back of the universal elliptic curve with
\Gamma(M)
-level structure with its structural map
\tilde{\pi}\colon\tcE{\longrightarrow\,}\bigsqcup\mathcal{H}
. This is the universal elliptic curve over
\bigsqcup\mathcal{H}
, or rather the disjoint union of
\varphi(M)
copies of the universal elliptic curve over
\mathcal{H}
. The latter can be described as the quotient
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\backslash(\mathbb{C}\times\mathcal{H})
, with
(m,n)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
acting on
(w,z)\in\mathbb{C}\times\mathcal{H}
via the rule

(m,n)\cdot(w,z):=(w+m+nz,z).

The fiber over
z\in\mathcal{H}
is clearly
\tilde{\pi}^{-1}(z)=\mathbb{C}/\langle 1,z\rangle
. Let
\tW^{0}_{2k-2}:=(\tcE)^{2k-2}
be the
(2k-2)
-fold fiber product of
\tcE
over
\bigsqcup\mathcal{H}
. It can be described as the disjoint union of copies of the quotient
(\mathbb{Z}^{2})^{(2k-2)}\backslash(\mathbb{C}^{2k-2}\times\mathcal{H})
. We also define
\tX^{0}:=(\tW^{0}_{2k-2}\times_{H}A^{2t})(\mathbb{C})
. We then have
W^{0}_{2k-2}(\mathbb{C})=\Gamma(M)\backslash\tW^{0}_{2k-2}
and
X^{0}(\mathbb{C})=\Gamma(M)\backslash\tX^{0}
, where
\Gamma(M)
acts on
\tcE^{0}
via

\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{matrix}\right)\cdot(w,z)=\left(\frac{w}{cz+d},\frac{az+b}{cz+d}\right).

Taking
n=2k-2
and
m=2t
, we define the PVHS

(33)

\tilde{\mathcal{W}}=\tilde{\mathcal{W}}_{k,t}:=\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{2k-2}(k-1)\otimes\kappa_{2t}H^{2t}_{\dR}(A^{2t}_{\mathbb{C}})(t)

with polarization inducing pairing

(34)

(\>,\>)_{k,t}^{0}:=(\>,\>)_{-2(k-1),-2t}\colon\tilde{\mathcal{W}}\times\tilde{\mathcal{W}}{\longrightarrow\,}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{H}}.

The underlying local system is

\tilde{W}=\tilde{\bL}^{B}_{2k-2,2t}(k+t-1).

6.4.
Connection with the formalism of Brylinski

Recall that
w:=a+ib
denotes the complex coordinate on
E_{z}(\mathbb{C}):=\tilde{\pi}^{-1}(z)=\mathbb{C}/\langle 1,z\rangle
.
Then
\underline{\omega}_{z}
is generated by
dw
and
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}(z)=H^{1}_{\dR}(E_{z})
admits the (canonical but non-holomorphic) Hodge decomposition

H^{1}_{\dR}(E_{z})=\mathbb{C}dw\oplus\mathbb{C}d\bar{w},\qquad H^{1,0}(E_{z})=\mathbb{C}dw,\qquad H^{0,1}(E_{z})=\mathbb{C}d\bar{w}.

Using the Poincaré pairing (
30
), we see that

(35)

(dw,d\bar{w})_{z}=\int_{E_{z}}dw\wedge d\bar{w}=-2i\int_{E_{z}}dadb=-(z-\bar{z}).

Let
p_{1}
and
p_{z}
denote the elements of
H_{1}(E_{z}(\mathbb{C}),\mathbb{Z})
corresponding to a closed path from
0
to
1
and
0
to
z
in
E_{z}(\mathbb{C})
respectively. Write
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{z}
for the elements in
H^{1}(E_{z}(\mathbb{C}),\mathbb{Q})
, which when viewed in
H_{\dR}^{1}(E_{z})
satisfy

(\omega,\eta_{1})_{z}=\int_{p_{1}}\omega,\qquad(\omega,\eta_{z})_{z}=\int_{p_{z}}\omega,\qquad\forall\omega\in H^{1}_{\dR}(E_{z}),

with respect to the Poincaré pairing (
30
).
Then
(dw,\eta_{1})_{z}=1,(dw,\eta_{z})_{z}=z
,
(d\bar{w},\eta_{1})_{z}=1
, and
(d\bar{w},\eta_{z})_{z}=\bar{z}
.
It follows from (
35
) that

(36)

dw=z\eta_{1}-\eta_{z}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad d\bar{w}=\bar{z}\eta_{1}-\eta_{z}.

Define the skew-symmetric pairing

(\>,\>)_{-1}\colon H^{1}(E_{z}(\mathbb{C}),\mathbb{R})\times H^{1}(E_{z}(\mathbb{C}),\mathbb{R}){\longrightarrow\,}\mathbb{R}(-1),\qquad(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})_{-1}:=\frac{1}{2\pi i}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})_{z}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{E_{z}}\omega_{1}\wedge\omega_{2}.

Then with respect to the basis
\{\eta_{1},-\eta_{z}\}
,
H^{1}(E_{z}(\mathbb{C}),\mathbb{R})
is a
2
-dimensional real vector space with a skew-symmetric bilinear pairing given by
(\eta_{1},-\eta_{z})_{-1}=-1/2\pi i
.

Following Brylinski
[
10
]
,
we now consider the standard representation
V=\mathbb{R}^{2}
of
G:={\rm GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})^{+}
with basis
\{u_{1},u_{2}\}
. We denote the representation by
\rho\colon G{\longrightarrow\,}{\rm GL}(V)
. In detail, we let
g=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in G
act on
v:=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=\lambda_{1}u_{1}+\lambda_{2}u_{2}\in V
via

g\cdot e=\rho(g)(e)=\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}\lambda_{1}\\
\lambda_{2}\end{matrix}\right)=\left(\begin{matrix}a\lambda_{1}+b\lambda_{2}\\
c\lambda_{1}+d\lambda_{2}\end{matrix}\right)=(a\lambda_{1}+b\lambda_{2})u_{1}+(c\lambda_{1}+d\lambda_{2})u_{2}.

The group
G
acts on the upper half-plane via

g\cdot z=\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{matrix}\right)\cdot z=\frac{az+b}{cz+d}.

Let
K\subset G
denote the stabilizer of
i\in\mathcal{H}
. Then
K
consists of matrices
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
-b&a\end{smallmatrix}\right)
such that
a^{2}+b^{2}\neq 0
, hence is identified with
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
by sending such a matrix to
a+bi
. The upper-half plane
\mathcal{H}
is thus identified with the quotient
G/\penalty K
. From this perspective, it is clear that

\mathcal{V}:=G\times^{K}V=(G\times V)/\penalty[(gk,v)\sim(g,\rho(k)(v))]

is a
G
-equivariant vector bundle on
\mathcal{H}
. It is trivialized via the (not
G
-equivariant) holomorphic identification

\varphi\colon\mathcal{H}\times V\overset{\sim}{{\longrightarrow\,}}\mathcal{V},\qquad(gK,v)\mapsto[(g,\rho(g)^{-1}(v))].

The
C^{\infty}
-sections of
\mathcal{V}
are the
C^{\infty}
-functions
F\colon G{\longrightarrow\,}V
satisfying
F(gk)=\rho(k)^{-1}(F(g))
for all
g\in G
,
k\in K
. In particular, the functions

u_{i}\colon G{\longrightarrow\,}V,\qquad g\mapsto\rho(g)^{-1}(u_{i}),\qquad i=1,2,

are global sections of
\mathcal{V}
(note the slight abuse of notation).
The holomorphic subbundle
\Fil^{1}\mathcal{V}
is generated by the nowhere vanishing holomorphic section
zu_{1}+u_{2}
.
Define a skew-symmetric bilinear pairing
(\>,\>)\colon V\times V{\longrightarrow\,}\mathbb{R}(-1)
by
(u_{1},u_{2})=-1/2\pi i
. Then the map

(37)

(V,(\>,\>))\overset{\sim}{{\longrightarrow\,}}(H^{1}(\tilde{\pi}^{-1}(z),\mathbb{R}),(\>,\>)_{-1}),\qquad\{u_{1},u_{2}\}\mapsto\{\eta_{1},-\eta_{z}\}

is an isomorphism of real vector spaces endowed with bilinear forms.

The pairing (
31
) is a polarization in the sense of Definition
6.1
. If we define
\langle\>,\>\rangle
on
\mathcal{V}_{z}\times\mathcal{V}_{z}
by
\langle v,w\rangle:=i^{p-q}2\pi i(v,\bar{w})
for
v\in\mathcal{V}_{z}^{p,q}
, then we recover Brylinski’s
[
10
]
calculation that

\displaystyle\langle zu_{1}+u_{2},zu_{1}+u_{2}\rangle_{z}

\displaystyle=2y

\displaystyle\langle zu_{1}+u_{2},\bar{z}u_{1}+u_{2}\rangle_{z}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\langle\bar{z}u_{1}+u_{2},\bar{z}u_{1}+u_{2}\rangle_{z}

\displaystyle=2y.

We conclude that

\left\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2y}}(zu_{1}+u_{2}),\frac{1}{\sqrt{2y}}(\bar{z}u_{1}+u_{2})\right\}

is an orthonormal
C^{\infty}
-basis of the vector bundle
\mathcal{V}^{\infty}={\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{\mathcal{H}}\otimes\mathcal{V}
with respect to the Hermitian form
\langle\>,\>\rangle

[
10
, Lem. 3.1]
.

Let
p\geq 1
and consider the local system
\sym^{2p}(V)
together with the pairing

(\>,\>)_{2p}\colon\sym^{2p}(V)\times\sym^{2p}(V){\longrightarrow\,}\mathbb{R}(-2p)

given by

(38)

(v_{1}\ldots v_{2p},w_{1}\ldots w_{2p})_{2p}:=\frac{1}{(2p)!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{2p}}(v_{1},w_{\sigma(1)})\ldots(v_{2p},w_{\sigma(2p)}).

Together with this pairing, the associated vector bundle
\sym^{2p}(\mathcal{V})
is a PVHS of weight
2p
in the sense of Definition
6.3
. The associated positive-definite Hermitian pairing

\langle\>,\>\rangle\colon\sym^{2p}(\mathcal{V})^{r,s}\times\sym^{2p}(\mathcal{V})^{r,s}{\longrightarrow\,}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{H}},

as in Remark
6.2
, is given by

\displaystyle\langle v_{1}\ldots v_{2p},w_{1}\ldots w_{2p}\rangle_{2p}

\displaystyle:=i^{r-s}(2\pi i)^{2p}(v_{1}\ldots v_{2p},\bar{w}_{1}\ldots\bar{w}_{2p})_{2p}

\displaystyle=\frac{i^{r-s}(2\pi i)^{2p}}{(2p)!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{2p}}(v_{1},\bar{w}_{\sigma(1)})\ldots(v_{2p},\bar{w}_{\sigma(2p)})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2p)!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{2p}}\langle v_{1},w_{\sigma(1)}\rangle\ldots\langle v_{2p},w_{\sigma(2p)}\rangle.

We now consider the PVHS
\mathcal{V}_{p}=\sym^{2p}(\mathcal{V})(p)
of weight
0
with polarization inducing pairing
(\>,\>)_{2p}^{0}\colon\mathcal{V}_{p}\times\mathcal{V}_{p}{\longrightarrow\,}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{H}}
defined by (
38
). Explicitly, we have

((2\pi i)^{p}v_{1}\ldots v_{2p},(2\pi i)^{p}w_{1}\ldots w_{2p})_{2p}^{0}:=\frac{(2\pi i)^{2p}}{(2p)!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{2p}}(v_{1},w_{\sigma(1)})\ldots(v_{2p},w_{\sigma(2p)}).

Note that the twists make sense (the pairing (
38
) takes values in
\mathbb{R}(-2p)
).
The associated Hermitian form
\langle\>,\>\rangle_{2p}^{0}\colon\mathcal{V}_{p}^{s,-s}\times\mathcal{V}_{p}^{s,-s}{\longrightarrow\,}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{H}}
of Definition
6.1
is

\displaystyle\langle(2\pi i)^{p}v_{1}\ldots v_{2p},(2\pi i)^{p}w_{1}\ldots w_{2p}\rangle_{2p}^{0}

\displaystyle:=(i)^{2s}((2\pi i)^{p}v_{1}\ldots v_{2p},(2\pi i)^{p}\bar{w}_{1}\ldots\bar{w}_{2p})_{2p}^{0}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{s}\frac{(2\pi i)^{2p}}{(2p)!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{2p}}(v_{1},\bar{w}_{\sigma(1)})\ldots(v_{2p},\bar{w}_{\sigma(2p)})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2p)!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{2p}}\langle v_{1},w_{\sigma(1)}\rangle\ldots\langle v_{2p},w_{\sigma(2p)}\rangle.

We then calculate

\left\langle(2\pi i)^{p}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2y}}(zu_{1}+u_{2})\right)^{n}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2y}}(\bar{z}u_{1}+u_{2})\right)^{2p-n},\right.\\
\left.(2\pi i)^{p}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2y}}(zu_{1}+u_{2})\right)^{n}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2y}}(\bar{z}u_{1}+u_{2})\right)^{2p-n}\right\rangle_{2p}^{0}=\binom{2p}{n}^{-1}.

It follows that the global sections

v_{n}:=\frac{\binom{2p}{n}^{1/2}(2\pi i)^{p}}{(2y)^{p}}(zu_{1}+u_{2})^{n}(\bar{z}u_{1}+u_{2})^{2p-n},

for
0\leq n\leq 2p
, form an orthonormal
C^{\infty}
-basis of
\mathcal{V}_{p}^{\infty}:={\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{\mathcal{H}}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{p}
with respect to the positive-definite Hermitian form
\langle\>,\>\rangle_{p}
, and each
v_{n}
is pure of type
(n-p,p-n)
.

Let
\tau_{0}:=x_{0}+iy_{0}\in\mathcal{H}
represent the fixed CM elliptic curve
